Quantum Representation Theory and Manin matrices I: finite-dimensional case
Alexey Silantyev

Abstract
We construct Quantum Representation Theory which describes quantum analogue of representations in frame of ‘non-commutative linear geometry’ developed by Manin in [ Man88 ] . To do it we generalise the internal hom-functor to the case of adjunction with a parameter and construct a general approach to representations of a monoid in a symmetric monoidal category with a parameter subcategory. Quantum Representation Theory is obtained by application of this approach to a monoidal category of some class of graded algebras with Manin product, where the parameter subcategory consists of connected finitely generated quadratic algebras. We formulate this theory in the language of Manin matrices and obtain quantum analogues of direct sum and tensor product of representations. Finally, we give some examples of quantum representations.



Bogoliubov Laboratory of Theoretical Physics, Joint Institute for Nuclear Research, 141980 Dubna, Moscow region, Russia

State University “Dubna”, Universitetskaya st. 19, 141980 Dubna, Moscow region, Russia

Keywords:
quadratic algebras; Manin matrices; quantum groups; non-commutative spaces; Representation Theory; monoidal categories.

1
Introduction

In the end of 80s Yuri Manin generalised the notion of finite-dimensional vector (linear) space to the quantum
1
1

1

We use the word ‘quantum’ instead of ‘non-commutative’ in the sense of non-commutative spaces by following Manin
[
Man88
]
and Drinfeld
[
Dr
]
, because sometimes the word ‘non-commutative’ is confusing. For example, the term ‘non-commutative group’ does not mean a non-commutative space with a group structure (i.e. a quantum group), but rather a non-abelian group. Moreover, we want to consider the usual ‘commutative’ spaces as a particular case of the non-commutative ones.

case
[
Man88
]
. He introduced the term ‘quantum linear spaces’ for (finitely generated connected) quadratic algebras considered as objects of the opposite category. The usual finite-dimensional vector spaces correspond to the polynomial algebras. Manin defined four binary operations with quadratic algebras, they where denoted by
\circ
,
\bullet
,
\otimes
,
\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}
. He also introduced a duality of quadratic algebras, which coincides with the Koszul duality for the quadratic Koszul algebras (see
[
Man87
,
Man88
,
Man91
]
).

The most important binary operation for our purposes is ‘
\circ
’, which we call ‘Manin white product’ or just ‘Manin product’. As it was shown in
[
Man88
]
the category of quantum linear spaces with these product is a closed monoidal category and that the internal hom can be written via the Koszul duality and the binary operation ‘
\bullet
’, which is called ‘black Manin product’. This gives a construction of the internal hom-object as a quadratic algebra generated by entries of some matrix.

It turned out that the description of the internal hom-object for the polynomial algebras gives some non-trivial commutation relations for the entries of this matrix. The matrices (over a non-commutative ring in general) satisfying these commutation relations were called ‘Manin matrices’ in
[
CF
]
, where the properties of these matrices and their determinants were applied to Talalaev’s formula
[
Tal
]
and many other questions. A lot of interesting properties were studied in details in
[
CFR
]
. The theory of Manin matrices was applied to Sugawara operators in
[
CM
]
and to some elliptic integrable systems in
[
RST
]
.

In the work
[
CFRS
]
the properties of Manin matrices were generalised for the
q
-case, where
q
is a parameter of the deformation of the polynomial algebras. The MacMahon Master Theorem and Cayley–Hamilton–Newton identities for the
q
-Manin matrices were obtained in
[
GLZ
,
FH07
,
FH08
]
and
[
IO
]
. Some properties and applications of the super-version of Manin matrices were derived in
[
MR
]
. Manin matrices for the series
B,C,D
appeared in the book
[
Molev
]
. Note also that the super-case with multi-parametric deformation was considered by Manin
[
Man89
,
Man91
,
Man92
]
.

The notion of Manin matrices was generalised in the work
[
S
]
in terms of idempotent operators (in
[
IO
]
these matrices were called ‘half-quantum matrices’). It was shown that any quadratic algebra can be given by a matrix idempotent
A
acting in a tensor product of a vector space. This algebra was denoted by
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
, where
{\mathbb{K}}
is a basic field. Then it was defined a Manin matrix for a pair of idempotents
A
and
B
and showed how this notion can be associated to the pair of quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
and
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
.

The main aim of this paper is to generalise Representation Theory to the case of a quantum representation space. The objects which are to be represented (such as algebras, groups, algebraic groups) are generalised up to ‘quantum algebras’ (these include quantum groups).

To do it we formulate a general representation theory in a monoidal category with some generalisation of internal hom-functor. For such category the representations of a monoid can be functorially identified with left actions of this monoid on some objects. In a particular case this approach gives Quantum Representation Theory: we need to take the monoidal category of quadratic algebras with the Manin product ‘
\circ
’ together with some extension of this category, where the category of quadratic algebras plays the role of parameter subcategory. This extension is needed to include the representations of quantum groups. As a ‘classical’ representation can be given by a square matrix in a basis of the representation space, a quantum representation is given by a Manin matrix for the pair of idempotents
A
and
B
, where
A=B
.

In the non-commutative geometry there is a widely known quantum analogue of actions of affine algebraic groups on algebraic sets – comodule algebras (see e.g.
[
Kass
, Def. III.7.1]
). The quantum representations can be considered as their ‘linear’ version, i.e. as a particular case of comodule algebras equipped with an additional structure. The notion of linear representation was generalised for quantum groups in
[
RTF
, Def. 20]
. It is a special case of quantum representation, when the quantum representation space is given by the tensor algebra.

This work is mainly devoted to the finite-dimensional case. In the quantum level this means that the algebras
{\mathcal{A}}
we consider are finitely generated and, as consequence, all the graded components
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
of these algebras are finite-dimensional vector spaces. For wider generality we sometimes include the infinite-dimensional (infinitely generated) case into consideration, but here we do not define quantum representations on infinitely generated quadratic algebras.

The paper is organised as follows. In Section
2
we give basic notations and definitions including theory of monoids and their actions. In Section
3
we describe the general approach to construct representation theory in a monoidal category by using the generalised internal hom. Since it is convenient to work in an opposite category we also define the dual notions: generalised internal cohom, corepresentations etc. Section
4
prepares the necessary notions and facts for the quantum representation theory: the Manin’s concept of quantum linear spaces, operations with quadratic algebras, semi-linear spaces and its quantum version, the internal cohom for quadratic algebras and its generalisation. Section
5
is devoted to the description of the quantum representations, to the consideration of classical representations as quantum ones and to binary operations with the quantum representations. In Section
6
we give some examples of quantum representations.

Acknowledgements
.
The author is grateful to A. Isaev, Y. Manin for useful references and to E. Patrin for discussions. The author particularly appreciates advice and guidance from V. Rubtsov.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Vector spaces and algebras

Fist of all, let us fix some terms and notations that we use in this work.

2.1.1.

Basic field.
Let
{\mathbb{K}}
be an infinite field such that
\mathop{\mathrm{char}}\nolimits{\mathbb{K}}\neq 2
. All the vector spaces will be over
{\mathbb{K}}
. For two vector spaces
V
and
W
we denote by
V\otimes W=V\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}W
their tensor product over
{\mathbb{K}}
. For briefness we say ‘
algebra
’ for a unital associative algebra over
{\mathbb{K}}
and we suppose that algebra homomorphisms map unity to unity. The unity of an algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
we denote by
1
or
1_{\mathcal{A}}
.

2.1.2.

Notations for morphisms in a category.
We denote the composition of morphisms
f\colon Y\to Z
and
g\colon X\to Y
in a category
{\mathbf{C}}
by
f\cdot g
. We do not use the symbol ‘
\circ
’ for composition, because it will be used for the Manin product.

For a natural transformation
\theta
between functors
F
and
G
we sometimes omit the subscript of its components
\theta_{X}\colon FX\to GX
. In particular, for a morphism
f\colon Y\to Z
we denote by
f_{*}
and
f^{*}
the maps

\displaystyle f_{*}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Z),

\displaystyle f_{*}(g)=f\cdot g\colon X\to Z,

\displaystyle g\colon X\to Y,

(2.1)

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Z,X)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Y,X),

\displaystyle f^{*}(h)=h\cdot f\colon Y\to X,

\displaystyle h\colon Z\to X.

(2.2)

They are the components of the natural transformations
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(-,Y)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(-,Z)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Z,-)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Y,-)
respectively.

2.1.3.

Basis.
A
basis
of a vector space
V
is a map
e\colon I\to V
from a set
I
(which may be infinite) such that for any vector space
W
and any map
f\colon I\to W
there exists a unique linear map
h\colon V\to W
satisfying
h\cdot e=f
. The basis
e\colon I\to V
is denoted by
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
or
(e_{i})
, where
e_{i}
is the value of
e
on
i\in I
. In the case of the set
I=\{1,\ldots,n\}
we also use the notation
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
. One can check that
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
is a basis of
V
iff the elements
e_{i}\in V
are pairwise different, the set
\{e_{i}\mid i\in I\}=e(I)
is linear independent and any vector
v\in V
is a finite linear combination of the form
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}e_{i}
where
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}}
. Notice that the basis
(e_{i})
is not just a set, because the permutation of the elements
e_{i}
gives another basis
(e^{\prime}_{i})
with the same set
\{e^{\prime}_{i}\}=\{e_{i}\}
.

2.1.4.

Bimodules.
Let
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
{\mathfrak{S}}
be algebras. By an
({\mathfrak{R}},{\mathfrak{S}})
-bimodule we understand a vector space
M
that has a structures of left
{\mathfrak{R}}
-module and right
{\mathfrak{S}}
-module agreed with the structure of the vector space. In other words,
M
is an
({\mathfrak{R}},{\mathfrak{S}})
-bimodule in the ring-theoretic sense satisfying the condition
\alpha m=m\alpha

\;\forall\,\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}},m\in M
. We also use the term
{\mathfrak{R}}
-bimodule or two-sided
{\mathfrak{R}}
-module for
({\mathfrak{R}},{\mathfrak{R}})
-bimodule. For any vector space
V
the tensor product
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
has a structure of
{\mathfrak{R}}
-bimodule:
r_{1}(r_{2}\otimes v)=(r_{1}\otimes m)r_{2}=(r_{1}r_{2})\otimes v
,
r_{1},r_{2}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
,
v\in V
.

2.1.5.

Grading.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
be the monoid of natural numbers (including
0
). We call an
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
-graded vector space simply
graded vector space
. The
k
-th component of a graded vector space
V
is denoted by
V_{k}
, so that
V=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}V_{k}
. By a
graded algebra
we mean an algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
with a structure of graded vector space such that
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}{\mathcal{A}}_{l}\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{k+l}
for any
k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
. The component
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
is an algebra, so
{\mathcal{A}}
has a structure of an
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
-bimodule. Each component
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
is its
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
-submodule. Note that the tensor product
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
of an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
with a graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is a graded algebra with the components
({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})_{k}={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
.

2.1.6.

Connected affinely generated graded algebras.
A graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
is called
connected
if
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
. The most interesting graded algebras in the projective (non-commutative) geometry are the connected algebras generated by the subspace
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
. Let us call a graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}

affinely generated
if it is generated by the subspace
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\oplus{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
, so the connected graded algebras
{\mathcal{A}}
generated by
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
are exactly the connected affinely generated graded algebras. Let us call an affinely generated graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}

semi-connected
if it is isomorphic to the graded algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
for an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
and a connected affinely generated graded algebra
{\mathcal{B}}
(since
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
we have
{\mathfrak{R}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
, so that
{\mathcal{A}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
).

2.1.7.

Quadratic algebras.
By a
quadratic algebra
over an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}
we mean an algebra isomorphic to
T_{\mathfrak{R}}M/\mathcal{I}
where

\displaystyle T_{\mathfrak{R}}M:={\mathfrak{R}}\oplus M\oplus\big(M\otimes_{{\mathfrak{R}}}M\big)\oplus\bigoplus\limits_{k\geqslant 3}\big(\underbrace{M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\cdots\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M}_{k}\big)

(2.3)

is the tensor algebra of an
{\mathfrak{R}}
-bimodule
M
and
\mathcal{I}
is a (two-sided) ideal of
T_{\mathfrak{R}}M
generated by a subset of
M\otimes_{{\mathfrak{R}}}M
. Any quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=T_{\mathfrak{R}}M/\mathcal{I}
has a structure of graded algebra with the components
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}={\mathfrak{R}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}=M
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}=M^{\otimes k}/({\mathcal{I}}\cap M^{\otimes k})
, where
M^{\otimes k}=M\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}\cdots\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}M
. Hence any quadratic algebra is affinely generated graded algebra.

In a narrow sense the notion ‘quadratic algebra’ is used for the quadratic algebras over
{\mathbb{K}}
. In this case the two-sided module
M
is a vector space
V
and
\alpha v=v\alpha
for any
\alpha\in{\mathbb{K}}
and
v\in V
. Such a quadratic algebra is isomorphic to
TV/I
, where
TV=T_{\mathbb{K}}V=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}V^{\otimes k}
is the tensor algebra of the vector space
V
and
I
is the ideal of
TV
generated by a subspace
R\subset V\otimes V
. These quadratic algebras are exactly the connected quadratic algebras.

Any semi-connected quadratic algebra over
{\mathfrak{R}}
is isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes(TV/I)\cong T_{\mathfrak{R}}M/\mathcal{I}
where
V
is a vector space,
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
,
I
and
\mathcal{I}
are the ideals of
TV
and
T_{\mathfrak{R}}M
generated by a subspace
R\subset V^{\otimes 2}
(over
{\mathbb{K}}
and over
{\mathfrak{R}}
respectively).

2.1.8.

Notations for some categories.

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Set}}

the category of sets,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Vect}}

the category of vector spaces (
{\mathbb{K}}
-modules),

\displaystyle{\mathbf{FVect}}

the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{GrVect}}

the category of graded vector spaces,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Alg}}

the category of algebras,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{FAlg}}

the category of finite-dinetional algebras,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{CommAlg}}

the category of commutative algebras,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{GrAlg}}

the category of graded algebras,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{QA}}

the category of quadratic algebras over
{\mathbb{K}}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{FQA}}

its full subcategory of
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
such that
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}

the category of quadratic algebras
{\mathcal{A}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
where
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}

the category of quadratic algebras
{\mathcal{A}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
where
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
.

The categories
{\mathbf{QA}}
and
{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
consist of the connected and semi-connected quadratic algebras respectively. The subcategory
{\mathbf{FQA}}\subset{\mathbf{QA}}
consists of finitely generated quadratic algebras
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
, while
{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}\subset{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
consists of algebras
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
finitely generated over
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
. Note that a quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)\in{\mathbf{QA}}
, where
(R)
is the ideal generated by a subspace
R\subset V^{\otimes 2}
, belongs to
{\mathbf{FQA}}
iff
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
. We obtain the following picture:

\textstyle{{\mathbf{FVect}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Vect}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{FAlg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{Alg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{CommAlg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{GrVect}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{GrAlg}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{FQA}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{QA}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where ‘
\hookrightarrow
’ are inclusions of full subcategories (fully faithful functors) and ‘
\to
’ are ‘forgetful’ functors (they are faithful, but not full).

2.2
Algebraic sets and affine schemes

Let us introduce some notions from algebraic geometry
[
Perr
]
,
[
Ful
]
,
[
Man70
]
,
[
Har
]
.

2.2.1.

Algebraic sets.
Define
(affine) algebraic set
(over
{\mathbb{K}}
) as the set of all the solutions of a system of algebraic equations with coefficients in
{\mathbb{K}}
, that is a set of all
n
-tuples
(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})\in{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
satisfying

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})=0,

(2.4)

where
F_{\alpha}\colon{\mathbb{K}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{K}}
are polynomial functions of
n
variables (with coefficients in
{\mathbb{K}}
) and
\alpha
runs over some set of indices. If the system (
2.4
) is empty then the corresponding algebraic set consists of all the
n
-tuples; it is denoted by
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}={\mathbb{A}}^{n}_{\mathbb{K}}
and called
affine space
. As a set the affine space
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
coincides with the vector space
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, but we consider it as an object of another category, where there are more morphisms. Namely, a morphism of affine spaces
\Phi\colon{\mathbb{A}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{m}
is a map
(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})\mapsto(P_{1}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}),\ldots,P_{m}\big(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})\big)
, where
P_{i}
are polynomial functions.

2.2.2.

Category of algebraic sets.
Any algebraic set is a subset of
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
for a certain
n
. A morphism between algebraic sets
X\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
and
Y\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{m}
is a map
\varphi\colon X\to Y
induced by a morphism
\Phi\colon{\mathbb{A}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{m}
in the sense that the diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}^{m}}

commutes. Denote the category of algebraic sets over
{\mathbb{K}}
with these morphisms by
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
.

2.2.3.

Algebra of regular functions.
A
regular function
on an algebraic set
X
is a morphism
X\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
. Since
{\mathbb{A}}^{1}={\mathbb{K}}
, the regular functions on
X
form an algebra, denote it by
A(X)
. The algebra of regular functions on
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
is the algebra of polynomials
{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
. By setting
Y={\mathbb{A}}^{m}={\mathbb{A}}^{1}={\mathbb{K}}
in the diagram (
2.2
) we see that a function
f\colon X\to{\mathbb{K}}
is regular iff there exists a polynomial
F\in{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
such that
f(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})=F(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
for any
(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})\in X
. We obtain an algebra epimorphism
{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]\twoheadrightarrow A(X)
, so that
A(X)\cong{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]/I(X)
, where
I(X)\subset{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
is the ideal of polynomials
F\in{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
such that
F(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})=0
for any
(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})\in X
.

2.2.4.

Affine schemes.
For any morphism
\varphi\colon X\to Y
its pull-back
\varphi^{*}\colon A(Y)\to A(X)
,
\varphi^{*}(f)=f\cdot\varphi
, is an algebra homomorphism. It is known that by mapping
X\mapsto A(X)
,
\varphi\mapsto\varphi^{*}
we obtain a contravariant fully faithful functor
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}\to{\mathbf{CommAlg}}
. This means that the category
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
is embedded into
{\mathbf{CommAlg}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
. The notion of algebraic set over
{\mathbb{K}}
is generalised to the notion of
affine scheme over
{\mathbb{K}}
such that these schemes form the category
{\mathbf{AffSch}}={\mathbf{CommAlg}}^{op}
. The affine scheme corresponding to an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{CommAlg}}
is denoted by
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}
. (see e.g.
[
Man70
]
for details).

2.2.5.

Sheaves of modules and vector bundles.
Any quasi-coherent sheaf of modules on an affine scheme
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}
is uniquely determined by the
{\mathfrak{R}}
-module of its global sections. Moreover, the category of all quasi-coherent sheaves of modules is equivalent to the category of
{\mathfrak{R}}
-modules. A vector bundle on an affine scheme is a particular case of a (quasi-)coherent sheaf. A trivial vector bundle (free sheaf) on
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}
with a fibre
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
corresponds to the
{\mathfrak{R}}
-module
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
. If
V={\mathbb{K}}^{n}
and
{\mathfrak{R}}=A(X)
for
X\in{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
, then a global section is a morphism
X\to X\times{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
such that
x\mapsto\big(x,v(x)\big)
for a morphism
v\colon X\to{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
. These sections form an
A(X)
-module isomorphic to
A(X)\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, an element
a\otimes v\in A(X)\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
gives a section
x\mapsto\big(x,a(x)v\big)
.

2.2.6.

Quantum affine schemes.
It is natural to define the quantum or ‘non-commutative’ version of the category of affine schemes as
{\mathbf{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
(see e.g.
[
Dr
]
). Thus, quantum affine schemes are algebras
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
, their morphisms are reversed algebra homomorphisms. As an analogue of the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
one should regard the category of left, right or two-sided
{\mathfrak{R}}
-modules.

2.3
Monoidal categories and functors

Here we remind the basic concepts from the theory of monoidal categories
[
MacLane
]
,
[
Bor2
]
.

2.3.1.

Monoidal categories.
A
monoidal category
is a category
{\mathbf{C}}
equipped with a bifunctor
\otimes\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
(called
a monoidal product
),
a unit object

I\in{\mathbf{C}}
and natural isomorphisms
(X\otimes Y)\otimes Z\cong X\otimes(Y\otimes Z)
,
I\otimes X\cong X
and
X\otimes I\cong X
satisfying some conditions (see
[
MacLane
]
for details). It is called
strict monoidal
if these isomorphisms are identities. Due to
[
MacLane
, 11.3,Th. 1]
any monoidal category is monoidally equivalent to a strict monoidal category, therefore we can suppose without loss of generality that all considered monoidal categories are strict monoidal (if it is not, one can always insert necessary isomorphisms into diagrams and formulae). We write
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes,I)
or simply
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
, since the unit object is unique up to an isomorphism. In the second case we sometimes write
I_{\mathbf{C}}
for the unit object. Note that if
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is a monoidal category, then its opposite
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}=({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
is also a monoidal category with the same unit object
I_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}}=I_{\mathbf{C}}
. A subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
of a monoidal category
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes,I)
is called
monoidal subcategory
if
I\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
f\otimes g\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
for any morphisms
f,g
in
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
(in particular,
X\otimes Y\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}

\;\forall\,X,Y\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
).

2.3.2.

Examples of monoidal categories.

•

If there exists a terminal object and a product of any pair of objects in a category
{\mathbf{C}}
, then
({\mathbf{C}},\times)
is a monoidal category and its terminal object is its unit object (in this case
{\mathbf{C}}
is called
a category with finite products
, see p.
2.5
). In particular,
({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus)
and its subcategory
({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)
are monoidal categories with unit object
0
, where
V\oplus W
is the direct sum of the vector spaces
V
and
W
.

•

If there exists an initial object and a coproduct of any pair of objects in a category
{\mathbf{C}}
, then
({\mathbf{C}},\amalg)
is a monoidal category. For instance, the category
({\mathbf{CommAlg}},\otimes)
is monoidal, since the tensor product
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}
of commutative algebras
{\mathfrak{R}}
and
{\mathfrak{S}}
is their coproduct. The opposite monoidal category is
({\mathbf{AffSch}},\times)
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}\times\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{S}}=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}})
is the categorical product.

•

Also, the category
{\mathbf{Vect}}
and its subcategory
{\mathbf{FVect}}
are monoidal with respect to the usual tensor product
\otimes
. The unit object is the one-dimensional vector space
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{K}}^{1}
.

•

The tensor product
\otimes
gives a structure of monoidal category on
{\mathbf{GrVect}}
. The monoidal product of two graded vector spaces
V
and
W
is the usual tensor product
V\otimes W
with the components
(V\otimes W)_{k}=\bigoplus\limits_{l=0}^{k}V_{l}\otimes W_{k-l}
. The unit object is the vector space
{\mathbb{K}}
with vanishing components of non-zero order:
({\mathbb{K}})_{k}=\delta_{k0}{\mathbb{K}}
. In this way we obtain the monoidal category
({\mathbf{GrVect}},\otimes,{\mathbb{K}})
.

•

The tensor product of two algebras
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
is the vector space
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
with the multiplication
(a\otimes b)(a^{\prime}\otimes b^{\prime})=(aa^{\prime})\otimes(bb^{\prime})
. This gives the monoidal category
({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes,{\mathbb{K}})
.

•

In the same way we obtain the monoidal category
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\otimes,{\mathbb{K}})
. One can check that all the full subcategories of
{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
defined above are monoidal subcategories.

•

Another useful example of a monoidal product on
{\mathbf{GrVect}}
is
Manin product
. For two objects
V,W\in{\mathbf{GrVect}}
it is defined as the graded vector space
V\circ W
with the components

\displaystyle(V\circ W)_{k}=V_{k}\otimes W_{k}.

(2.9)

The monoidal category
({\mathbf{GrVect}},\circ)
is equivalent to the direct product
\prod\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
. Its unit object is a graded vector space with one-dimensional components, that is the polynomial algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[u]
with the standard grading. Note also that we have the (non-graded) inclusion of vector spaces
V\circ W\subset V\otimes W
.

•

One can show that for any
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
their Manin product
{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
is a subalgebra of
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
. Thus we obtain the monoidal category
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ,{\mathbb{K}}[u])
and its monoidal subcategories (the product ‘
\circ
’ was originally defined by Manin for the category
{\mathbf{FQA}}
).

2.3.3.

Monoidal functors.
Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be two monoidal categories. The functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
equipped with a morphism
\varphi\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FI_{\mathbf{C}}
and a natural transformation
\phi_{X,Y}\colon FX\odot FY\to F(X\otimes Y)
is called
lax monoidal functor
if for any
X,Y,Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
the diagrams

\textstyle{FX\odot FY\odot FZ\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\phi_{Y,Z}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X,Y}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{FX\odot F(Y\otimes Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X,Y\otimes Z}}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y)\odot FZ\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X\otimes Y,Z}}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y\otimes Z)}

\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{D}}\odot FX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{FX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F(I_{\mathbf{C}})\odot FX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{I_{\mathbf{C}},X}}
\textstyle{F(I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes X)}
\textstyle{FX\odot I_{\mathbf{D}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\odot\varphi}
\textstyle{FX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{FX\odot F(I_{\mathbf{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X,I_{\mathbf{C}}}}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}})}

commute. The functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is called
colax monoidal
if its opposite
F^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is lax monoidal. In other words, this is a triple
(F,\varphi,\phi)
, where
\varphi\colon FI_{\mathbf{C}}\to I_{\mathbf{D}}
and
\phi
is a natural transformation with components
\phi_{X,Y}\colon F(X\otimes Y)\to FX\odot FY
such that the reversed diagrams (
2.3
), (
2.3
) commute, i.e.
(\phi_{X,Y}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FZ})\cdot\phi_{X\otimes Y,Z}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FX}\odot\phi_{Y,Z})\cdot\phi_{X,Y\otimes Z}
,
(\varphi\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FX})\cdot\phi_{I_{\mathbf{C}},X}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FX}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FX}\odot\varphi)\cdot\phi_{X,I_{\mathbf{C}}}
.

A
strong monoidal functor
is a lax (or colax) monoidal functor
(F,\varphi,\phi)
such that
\varphi
and
\phi_{X,Y}
are isomorphisms. A strong monoidal functor is called
strict monoidal functor
if they are identities. For instance, the inclusion of a monoidal subcategory
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
into
{\mathbf{C}}
is a faithful strict monoidal functor. Note that a composition of two lax/colax/strong monoidal functors is a lax/colax/strong monoidal functor.

2.3.4.

Examples of monoidal functors.

•

Consider the dualisation functor
(-)^{*}\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
which sends a vector space
V\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
to its dual
V^{*}
, it is defined on linear maps
f\colon V\to W
by the formula (
2.2
). For any vector spaces
V,W\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
the tensor product
V^{*}\otimes W^{*}
is naturally embedded into
(V\otimes W)^{*}
, so the functor
(-)^{*}\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
has a structure of lax monoidal functor
({\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
. The opposite functor
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
is colax monoidal.

•

The same functor considered on finite-dimensional vector spaces is a strong monoidal functor
(-)^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
, since the embedding gives the natural isomorphisms
V^{*}\otimes W^{*}\cong(V\otimes W)^{*}
for any
V,W\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
.

•

Note that
(V\oplus W)^{*}\cong V^{*}\oplus W^{*}
for any
V,W\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
, so we obtain strong monoidal contravariant functors
(-)^{*}\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus)
and
(-)^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)
.

•

The functor
T\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
, which gives the tensor algebra
TV
of a vector space
V\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
, is a strong monoidal functor
T\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
. The isomorphisms
\phi_{V,W}\colon TV\circ TW\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}T(V\otimes W)
have the graded components

\displaystyle(\phi_{V,W})_{k}\colon V^{\otimes k}\otimes W^{\otimes k}\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}(V\otimes W)^{\otimes k},

(2.22)

\displaystyle v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{k}\otimes w_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes w_{k}\mapsto(v_{1}\otimes w_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes(v_{k}\otimes w_{k}).

It gives also the strong monoidal functors

\displaystyle T\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{QA}},\circ),

\displaystyle T\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}},\circ).

(2.23)

2.3.5.

Symmetric monoidal category.
A monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
equipped with a natural isomorphism
\sigma_{X,Y}\colon X\otimes Y\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}Y\otimes X
is called
symmetric
if for any
X,Y,Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
we have
\sigma_{Y,X}\sigma_{X,Y}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X\otimes Y}
and the diagrams

\textstyle{X\otimes Y\otimes Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{X,Y}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{X,Y\otimes Z}}
\textstyle{Y\otimes X\otimes Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\sigma_{X,Z}}
\textstyle{Y\otimes Z\otimes X}
\textstyle{X\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{X,I_{\mathbf{C}}}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes X}

commute. The natural transformation
\sigma
gives an additional structure on the monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}
.

All the monoidal categories considered in p.
2.3
are symmetric. For example, the symmetric structure of
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
is given by the isomorphisms

\displaystyle\sigma_{V,W}\colon V\otimes W\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}W\otimes V,

\displaystyle\sigma_{V,W}(v\otimes w)=w\otimes v.

(2.32)

One can check that it induces the symmetric structure of the monoidal categories
({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)
,
({\mathbf{GrVect}},\otimes)
,
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\otimes)
,
({\mathbf{GrVect}},\circ)
,
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
and of their monoidal subcategories considered above. In fact, we already used the maps (
2.32
): the natural isomorphisms (
2.22
) are compositions of some operators of the form
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{\otimes l}\otimes\sigma_{V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{\otimes(2k-l-2)}
,
l=1,\ldots,2k-3
,
k\geqslant 2
.

If a monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is symmetric, then
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}=({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
is also symmetric: the role of the structure isomorphisms
V\otimes W\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}W\otimes V
in
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is played by the structure isomorphisms
\sigma_{W,V}\colon W\otimes V\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}V\otimes W
of the category
{\mathbf{C}}
.

2.3.6.

Symmetric monoidal functors.
Let
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be symmetric monoidal categories. A lax (or strong) monoidal functor
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
with the structure natural transformation
\phi_{X,Y}\colon FX\odot FY\to F(X\otimes Y)
is called
symmetric
if it preserves the symmetric structure in the sense that all the diagrams

\textstyle{FX\odot FY\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{FX,FY}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{FY\odot FX\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{Y,X}}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F\sigma_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{F(Y\otimes X)}

commute. A colax monoidal functor
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
with the structure natural transformation
\phi_{X,Y}\colon F(X\otimes Y)\to FX\odot FY
is called
symmetric
if the diagrams (
2.3
) with the reversed vertical arrows commute (i.e. if the lax monoidal functor
F^{\mathrm{op}}\colon({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}}^{\mathrm{op}},\odot)
is symmetric). A composition of symmetric monoidal functors is symmetric. All the monoidal functors considered in p.
2.3
are symmetric.

2.4
Monoids and their actions

We define a notion of monoid, bimonoid and Hopf monoid for a general (symmetric) monoidal category
[
MacLane
]
,
[
Porst
]
. They generalise the notions of algebra, bialgebra and Hopf algebra (for this case see
[
Kass
]
).

2.4.1.

Monoids and comonoids.
A
monoid
in a monoidal category
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is a triple
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
of an object
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
with structure morphisms
\mu_{X}\colon X\otimes X\to X
and
\eta_{X}\colon I_{\mathbf{C}}\to X
such that the diagrams

\textstyle{X\otimes X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mu_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{I\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}}
\textstyle{X\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\eta_{X}}
\textstyle{X}

commute. Morphisms
\mu_{X}
and
\eta_{X}
, which give a structure of monoid on
X
, are often called
multiplication
and
unit
.

A
comonoid
in
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is a triple
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
of an object
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
and morphisms
\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X\otimes X
and
\varepsilon_{X}\colon X\to I_{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
such that the reversed diagrams (
2.4
) commute, i.e.
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes\Delta_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}=(\Delta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}
and
(\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes\varepsilon_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}
. For
\Delta_{X}
and
\varepsilon_{X}
one uses the terms ‘
comultiplication
’ and ‘
counit
’.

2.4.2.

Morphisms of monoids and comonoids.

A morphism of monoids
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
and
(Y,\mu_{Y},\eta_{Y})
or comonoids
(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
and
(Y,\Delta_{Y},\varepsilon_{Y})
is a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
{\mathbf{C}}
preserving the structure morphisms:

\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f\otimes f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{Y}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y}
or
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\otimes f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y\otimes Y}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}}
\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{Y}}

Since the composition of two morphisms of (co)monoids is a morphisms of (co)monoids, the monoids and comonoids in a monoidal category
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
form categories denoted by
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
respectively. Note that
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)=\big(\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)\big)^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Consider two monoids
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X}),(Y,\mu_{Y},\eta_{Y})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
or comonoids
(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X}),(Y,\Delta_{Y},\varepsilon_{Y})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
in a monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
. Let
f\colon X\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}Y
be an isomorphism in the category
{\mathbf{C}}
. If
f\colon X\to Y
preserves structure of these monoids or comonoids, then
f^{-1}\colon Y\to X
also preserves structure of these monoids or comonoids. In particular, this implies an isomorphism of the corresponding monoids or comonoids in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
or
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
respectively.

Proof.
It is enough to consider the case of monoids. By using the commutativity of the left diagrams (
2.4
) one yields
f^{-1}\cdot\eta_{Y}=f^{-1}\cdot f\cdot\eta_{X}=\eta_{X}
and
f^{-1}\cdot\mu_{Y}=f^{-1}\cdot\mu_{Y}\cdot(f\otimes f)\cdot(f^{-1}\otimes f^{-1})=f^{-1}\cdot f\cdot\mu_{X}\cdot(f^{-1}\otimes f^{-1})=\mu_{X}\cdot(f^{-1}\otimes f^{-1})
. ∎

2.4.3.

Monoidal products of monoids and comonoids.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is symmetric then
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
and
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
are monoidal categories and they are also symmetric: the monoidal product of monoids
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
and
(Y,\mu_{Y},\eta_{Y})
or comonoids
(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
and
(Y,\Delta_{Y},\varepsilon_{Y})
is the object
X\otimes Y
with the structure defined by the diagrams

\textstyle{X\otimes Y\otimes X\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\sigma_{Y,X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X\otimes Y}}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\otimes Y\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}\otimes\mu_{Y}}
\textstyle{X\otimes Y}

\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X\otimes Y}}
\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}\otimes\eta_{Y}}
\textstyle{X\otimes Y}

or

\textstyle{X\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}\otimes\Delta_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X\otimes Y}}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\otimes Y\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\sigma_{X,Y}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{X\otimes Y\otimes X\otimes Y}

\textstyle{X\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\varepsilon_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X\otimes Y}}
\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}}

A usual monoid is a monoid in the monoidal category
{\mathbf{Set}}=({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
. A monoid in
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
is an algebra:
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)=({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)
. In its subcategory of finite-dimensional vector spaces we have
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)=({\mathbf{FAlg}},\otimes)
. Comonoids in
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
are called
coalgebras
. Analogously, we have
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{GrVect}},\otimes)=({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\otimes)
.

2.4.4.

Eckmann–Hilton Principle.

A monoid
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
in a symmetric monoidal category
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is called
commutative
if
\mu_{X}\cdot\sigma_{X,X}=\mu_{X}
. Analogously, a comonoid
(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
is called
cocommutative
if
\sigma_{X,X}\cdot\Delta_{X}=\Delta_{X}
. Thus we obtain full monoidal subcategories
\mathop{\mathbf{{\vphantom{M}}{}_{c}Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\subset\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
\mathop{\mathbf{{\vphantom{C}}{}_{coc}Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\subset\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
. For example, for the category
{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{Vect}}
with the tensor product
\otimes
we have
\mathop{\mathbf{{\vphantom{M}}{}_{c}Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)=({\mathbf{CommAlg}},\otimes)
.

The Eckmann–Hilton Principle was formulated in
[
EH
, Th 1.12]
for the groups: if a group homomorphism
\mu\colon G\times G\to G
has a neutral element as a binary operation, then the group
G
is abelian and
\mu
coincides with the group multiplication. This fact is generalised for any symmetric monoidal category
({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
(see e.g.
[
Porst
, § 4]
).

Proposition 2.2
.

The category
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\big)
is equivalent to
\mathop{\mathbf{{\vphantom{M}}{}_{c}Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
. Namely, the monoid structure
(\mu_{\mathbb{M}},\eta_{\mathbb{M}})
on an object
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
exists iff the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
is commutative. This structure is unique:
\mu_{\mathbb{M}}=\mu_{X}
,
\eta_{\mathbb{M}}=\eta_{X}
.
The category
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\big)
is equivalent to
\mathop{\mathbf{{\vphantom{C}}{}_{coc}Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
in the same way.

2.4.5.

The functors
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)
and
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)
.

Any lax monoidal functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
between monoidal categories
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
induces the functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}}).

(2.61)

Namely, if
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
is a monoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
, then
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}=(FX,\mu_{FX},\eta_{FX})
is a monoid in
{\mathbf{D}}
, where
\mu_{FX}\colon FX\odot FX\to FX
and
\eta_{FX}\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FX
are the compositions

\displaystyle FX\odot FX\xrightarrow{\phi_{X,X}}F(X\otimes X)\xrightarrow{F\mu_{X}}FX

and

\displaystyle I_{\mathbf{D}}\xrightarrow{\varphi}FI_{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{F\eta_{X}}FX

(2.62)

respectively. Dually, a colax monoidal functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
induces the functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}}).

(2.63)

Note that the functors (
2.61
) and (
2.63
) depend on the monoidal structure on
F
, that is
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F,\varphi,\phi)
and
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F,\varphi,\phi)
.

A faithful lax or colax monoidal functor
F
induces a faithful functor (
2.61
) or (
2.63
) respectively. A strong monoidal functor
F
gives strong monoidal functors (
2.61
) and (
2.63
). If
F
is strong monoidal and fully faithful, then (
2.61
) and (
2.63
) are also fully faithful (the condition to be strong monoidal can be weakened: it is enough to require
\varphi
and all
\phi_{X,X}
to be epimorphisms/monomorphisms for lax/colax case).

Suppose the categories
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
are symmetric. If
F
is a symmetric lax/strong monoidal functor, then
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)
is a symmetric lax/strong monoidal functor as well. If
F
is a symmetric colax/strong monoidal functor, then
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)
is also a symmetric colax/strong monoidal functor.

2.4.6.

Bimonoids.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
be a symmetric monoidal category. A
bimonoid
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
such that
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
and
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
are monoid and comonoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
with compatible structures:

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-20.19997pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern 44.19997pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 74.19997pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(2.64)

These diagrams mean exactly that
\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X\otimes X
and
\varepsilon_{X}\colon X\to I_{\mathbf{C}}
are monoid morphisms
(X\otimes X,\mu_{X\otimes X},\eta_{X\otimes X})\to(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
and
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\to(I_{\mathbf{C}},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}})
or, equivalently, that
\mu_{X}\colon X\otimes X\to X
and
\varepsilon_{X}\colon I_{\mathbf{C}}\to X
are comonoid morphisms
(X\otimes X,\Delta_{X\otimes X},\varepsilon_{X\otimes X})\to(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
and
(I_{\mathbf{C}},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}})\to(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
. Bimonoids in
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
are objects of the symmetric monoidal category
\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)=\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\big)=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\big)
.

Any symmetric strong monoidal functor
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
between symmetric monoidal categories
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{D}}
induces the symmetric strong monoidal functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}}),

(2.65)

where
\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(F)=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)\big)=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\big)
.

2.4.7.

Hopf monoids.
For arbitrary bimonoid
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
define a convolution on the set
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}(X)
. The convolution of two morphisms
\alpha\colon X\to X
and
\beta\colon X\to X
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is the morphism
\alpha\star\beta:=\mu_{X}\cdot(\alpha\otimes\beta)\cdot\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X
. This is a multiplication on the set
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}(X)
with the neutral element
\eta_{X}\cdot\varepsilon_{X}
. A
Hopf monoid
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is a bimonoid
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
in
{\mathbf{C}}
such that the morphism
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}
has an inverse
\zeta_{X}\colon X\to X
with respect to the convolution, that is
\zeta_{X}\star\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\star\zeta_{X}=\eta_{X}\cdot\varepsilon_{X}
. The morphism
\zeta_{X}\colon X\to X
is called
antipode
. It is unique since the convolution is associative.

The functor (
2.65
) maps a Hopf monoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
to a Hopf monoid in
{\mathbf{D}}
, the antipode
\zeta_{X}\colon X\to X
is mapped to the antipode
F(\zeta_{X})\colon FX\to FX
.

Bimonoids and Hopf monoids in
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
are
bialgebras
and
Hopf algebras
respectively.

2.4.8.

Opposite monoid and coopposite comonoid.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
be a monoid in a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
. Let
\mu_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}:=\mu_{X}\cdot\sigma_{X,X}\colon X\otimes X\to X
be
opposite multiplication
. Then
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{op}}:=(X,\mu_{X}^{\mathrm{op}},\eta_{X})
is also monoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
, it is called
monoid opposite to

{\mathbb{M}}
. Note that
{\mathbb{M}}
is commutative in the sense of p.
2.4
iff
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{op}}={\mathbb{M}}
, but in general these are different monoids. If
f\colon X\to\widetilde{X}
is a morphism of the monoids
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=(\widetilde{X},\mu_{\widetilde{X}},\eta_{\widetilde{X}})
, then it is a morphism
{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}^{\mathrm{op}}
as well.

Dually, let
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
be a comonoid in a monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
. The comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}^{\mathrm{cop}}:=(X,\Delta_{X}^{\mathrm{cop}},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
with the so-called
coopposite comultiplication

\Delta^{\mathrm{cop}}=\sigma_{X,X}\cdot\Delta
and the same counit
\varepsilon_{X}
is called
comonoid coopposite to

{\mathbb{O}}
.

For a bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
we can define the following tree bimonoids:
opposite bimonoid

{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}=(X,\mu_{X}^{\mathrm{op}},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
,
coopposite bimonoid

{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathrm{cop}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X}^{\mathrm{cop}},\varepsilon_{X})
and
opposite coopposite bimonoid

{\mathbb{B}}^{{\mathrm{op}},{\mathrm{cop}}}=(X,\mu_{X}^{\mathrm{op}},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X}^{\mathrm{cop}},\varepsilon_{X})
(the commutativity of the corresponding diagrams (
2.64
) is check straightforwardly). Moreover, if the bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}
is a Hopf monoid, then
{\mathbb{B}}^{{\mathrm{op}},{\mathrm{cop}}}
is also a Hopf monoid with the same antipode.

2.4.9.

Actions and coactions.
A
(left) action
of a monoid
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
on an object
V\in{\mathbf{C}}
is a morphism
a\colon X\otimes V\to V
making the diagrams

\textstyle{X\otimes X\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes a}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}}
\textstyle{X\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{X\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{V}

\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}}
\textstyle{X\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{V}

commutative. A
(left) coaction
of a comonoid
(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
on an object
V\in{\mathbf{C}}
is a morphism
\delta\colon V\to X\otimes V
making the reversed diagrams commutative:

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{X\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}}
\textstyle{X\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes\delta}
\textstyle{X\otimes X\otimes V}

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{X\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}}
\textstyle{I_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes V}

The actions of a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
or a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
form a category
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
or
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}})
respectively. Their objects are pairs
(V,a)
and
(V,\delta)
. A morphism
(V,a)\to(V^{\prime},a^{\prime})
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
or
(V,\delta)\to(V^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
is a morphism
f\colon V\to V^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{X\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{X\otimes V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a^{\prime}}
\textstyle{V^{\prime}}

or

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{X\otimes V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes f}
\textstyle{V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\otimes V^{\prime}}

commutes. For the case
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
is an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
and the objects of
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{R}})
are exactly
{\mathfrak{R}}
-modules, while
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{C}})
is the category of comodules of a coalgebra
{\mathfrak{C}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
.

2.4.10.

Actions and coactions of a bimonoid.

A bimonoid in a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. By considering it as the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
we obtain the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}}):=\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
. The comonoid structure
(\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
turns
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
into a monoidal category in the following way
[
Par
]
. Let
(V,a)
and
(W,b)
be two objects of
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
. Their monoidal product
(V,a)\otimes(W,b)
is the object
V\otimes W
with the action

\displaystyle X\otimes(V\otimes W)\xrightarrow{\Delta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}X\otimes X\otimes V\otimes W\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\sigma_{X,V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}X\otimes V\otimes X\otimes W\xrightarrow{a\otimes b}V\otimes W.

(2.90)

If
f\colon V\to V^{\prime}
and
g\colon W\to W^{\prime}
are morphisms in
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
of the forms
(V,a)\to(V^{\prime},a^{\prime})
and
(W,b)\to(W^{\prime},b^{\prime})
, then their monoidal product
f\otimes g\colon V\otimes W\to V^{\prime}\otimes W^{\prime}
is a morphism
(V,a)\otimes(W,b)\to(V^{\prime},a^{\prime})\otimes(W^{\prime},b^{\prime})
.
The unit object of
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
is
I_{\mathbf{C}}
with the action
X\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}}=X\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{X}}I_{\mathbf{C}}
. If
{\mathbb{B}}
is cocommutative (
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathrm{cop}}={\mathbb{B}}
), then the symmetric structure on
{\mathbf{C}}
gives a symmetric structure on the monoidal category
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
.

Dually, the structure
(\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
turns the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}}):=\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
into monoidal category. If
\delta
and
\gamma
are coactions of the comonoid
(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
on
V
and
W
respectively, then its coaction on
V\otimes W
is the composition

\displaystyle V\otimes W\xrightarrow{\delta\otimes\gamma}X\otimes V\otimes X\otimes W\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\sigma_{V,X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}X\otimes X\otimes V\otimes W\xrightarrow{\mu_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}X\otimes(V\otimes W).

(2.91)

The coaction on
I_{\mathbf{C}}
is the morphism
I_{\mathbf{C}}\xrightarrow{\eta_{X}}X=X\otimes I_{\mathbf{C}}
. If
{\mathbb{B}}
is commutative (
{\mathbb{B}}^{\mathrm{op}}={\mathbb{B}}
), then
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})
is a symmetric monoidal category.

2.4.11.

Translation of actions and coactions.

Let
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be a lax monoidal functor with structure morphisms
\phi_{X,Y}\colon FX\odot FY\to F(X\otimes Y)
,
\varphi\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FI_{\mathbf{C}}
. The functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
maps a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
to the monoid
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)({\mathbb{M}})=(FX,\mu_{FX},\eta_{FX})
, where
\mu_{FX}\colon FX\odot FX\to FX
,
\eta_{FX}\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FX
are (
2.62
). Let
a\colon X\otimes V\to V
be an action of the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
on
V
and consider the morphism
\widetilde{a}\colon FX\odot FV\to FV
defined as a composition

\displaystyle FX\odot FV\xrightarrow{\phi_{X,V}}F(X\otimes V)\xrightarrow{F(a)}FV.

(2.92)

It is straightforward to check that
\widetilde{a}
is an action of
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}
on
FV
. If
f\colon V\to V^{\prime}
is a morphism
(V,a)\to(V^{\prime},a^{\prime})
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
then
F(f)\colon FV\to FV^{\prime}
is a morphism between the corresponding actions in
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}})
. Thus we obtain a functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}})

(2.93)

induced by the lax monoidal functor
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)
.

Dually, a colax monoidal functor
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
induces the functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}})

(2.94)

for comonoids
{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
.

If the monoidal categories
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
are symmetric, then any symmetric strong monoidal functor
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
induces the strong monoidal functors

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}),

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{B}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}})

(2.95)

for bimonoids
{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{B}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
.

2.5
Monoids and groups in a category with finite products

A category with finite products is an important case of monoidal category. Here we consider this case in details (see
[
MacLane
, § 3.5, 3.6]
).

2.5.1.

Category with finite products.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a category. Suppose that there exists a terminal object
E
and a categorical product
X\times Y
of any two objects
X,Y\in{\mathbf{C}}
. Then there exist all the
finite products

\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
, where
X_{i}\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
(for
n=0
this product is equal to
E
). In this case
({\mathbf{C}},\times)
is a symmetric monoidal category
[
MacLane
, § 7.7]
. For morphisms
f\colon Y\to X_{1}
and
g\colon Y\to X_{2}
we denote by
(f,g)
the unique morphism
Y\to X_{1}\times X_{2}
making the diagram

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{(f,g)}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\times X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{X_{2}}

commutative, where
p_{1}\colon X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X_{1}
and
p_{2}\colon X_{1}\times X_{2}\to X_{2}
are canonical projections of the product
X_{1}\times X_{2}
. More generally, for
f_{i}\colon Y\to X_{i}
there is a unique morphism
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\colon Y\to\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
such that
p_{i}\cdot f=f_{i}
, where
p_{i}\colon\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\to X_{i}
are canonical projections.

2.5.2.

Diagonal morphism.
Any object
X
of a category with finite products
{\mathbf{C}}
is equipped with a structure of comonoid in a unique way. Indeed, since the object
E
is terminal, there is a unique morphism
\varepsilon_{X}\colon X\to E
. The morphisms
X\times X\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\times\varepsilon_{X}}X\times E\cong X
and
X\times X\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{X}\times\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}}E\times X\cong X
coincide with the canonical projections
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
respectively, so the morphism
\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X\times X
can be uniquely found from the commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}}
\textstyle{X\times E}
\textstyle{X\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\times\varepsilon_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}\times\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}}
\textstyle{E\times X}

This is
\Delta_{X}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X})\colon X\to X\times X
, it is called
diagonal morphism
for the object
X
. One can check that the composition of both morphisms
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\times\Delta_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X\times X\times X
and
(\Delta_{X}\times\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X\times X\times X
with all three canonical projections
X\times X\times X\to X
are equal to
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}
. Thus they coincide with the morphism
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X},\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X})
and hence equal to each other. Note that the diagonal morphism is cocommutative:
\Delta_{X}^{\mathrm{op}}=\Delta_{X}
.

In this way we obtain a unique comonoid
(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\times)
for any object
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
. Moreover, any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is a morphism of comonoids
(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\to(Y,\Delta_{Y},\varepsilon_{Y})
. This means that
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\times)
coincide with
{\mathbf{C}}
as a category. Since the monoidal product in
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\times)
coincide with the direct product
\times
in
{\mathbf{C}}
, we have the strict monoidal equivalence

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\times)=\mathop{\mathbf{{\vphantom{C}}{}_{coc}Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\times)=({\mathbf{C}},\times).

(2.104)

2.5.3.

Monoids and groups in the category
{\mathbf{C}}
.
A monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
in
({\mathbf{C}},\times)
is called
monoid in the category

{\mathbf{C}}
. Due to the equivalence (
2.104
) any structure of monoid on
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
is compatible with the structure
(\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
.
Hence the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
has a unique structure of bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\times)
given by the diagonal morphism
\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X\times X
and the unique morphism
\varepsilon_{X}\colon X\to E
, so we obtain the strict monoidal equivalence

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\times)=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\times).

(2.105)

Note also that any bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}},\times)
is cocommutative.

The category
{\mathbf{Set}}
is a category with finite products. The terminal object in
{\mathbf{Set}}
is the one-point set
E=\{*\}
. For a set
X\in{\mathbf{Set}}
the diagonal morphism
\Delta_{X}\colon X\to X\times X
has the form
\Delta_{X}(x)=(x,x)
and the morphism
\varepsilon_{X}\colon X\to E
maps any element
x\in X
to the unique element
*
of the set
E
. A monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
in
({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
is a monoid in the usual sense:
\mu_{X}(x,y)=xy
is a multiplication map and
\eta_{X}(*)=e
gives the neutral element
e\in X
. It becomes a bimonoid in a unique way:
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
. The convolution of two maps
\alpha,\beta\colon X\to X
is the point-wise product:
(\alpha\star\beta)(x)=\alpha(x)\beta(x)
; the neutral element for this convolution is the map
\eta_{X}\cdot\varepsilon_{X}\colon x\mapsto e
. Hence the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
is a group iff the bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
is a Hopf monoid in
({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
. The role of antipode is played by the map
\zeta_{X}(x)=x^{-1}
.

For general category with finite products
{\mathbf{C}}
the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is called
group in the category

{\mathbf{C}}
if the corresponding bimonoid
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
is a Hopf monoid in
({\mathbf{C}},\times)
. It is enough to require one of the conditions
\mu_{X}\cdot(\zeta_{X}\times\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}=\eta_{X}\cdot\varepsilon_{X}
or
\mu_{X}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\times\zeta_{X})\cdot\Delta_{X}=\eta_{X}\cdot\varepsilon_{X}
, since they are equivalent in this case.

2.5.4.

Algebraic monoids and algebraic groups.

The category
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
also has all finite products. Indeed, the terminal object is
{\mathbb{A}}^{0}
, the product of two algebraic sets
X\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
and
Y\subset{\mathbb{A}}^{m}
is the set-theoretic product
X\times Y
canonically embedded into
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{A}}^{m}={\mathbb{A}}^{n+m}
. As we already mentioned in p.
2.3
the category
{\mathbf{CommAlg}}
has all finite coproducts and hence
{\mathbf{AffSch}}={\mathbf{CommAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
has all finite products. Since the categorical product of the affine schemes
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{S}}
is
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}})
, we have
A(X\times Y)=A(X)\otimes A(Y)
for any
X,Y\in{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
.

An
(affine) algebraic monoid
is a monoid in
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
. Explicitly, this is a usual monoid that has a structure of algebraic set
X
such that the multiplication map
X\times X\to X
,
(x,y)\mapsto xy
, is a morphism in
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
.

An
(affine) algebraic group
is a group in
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
. This is a group with a structure of algebraic set
X
such that the maps
X\times X\to X
,
(x,y)\mapsto xy
, and
X\to X
,
x\mapsto x^{-1}
, are morphisms in
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
.

More generally, one can define
affine monoid/group scheme
as a monoid/group in
{\mathbf{AffSch}}
. Structure of monoid on an affine scheme
X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}
is given by the homomorphisms
\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathbb{K}}
making
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{CommAlg}}
into a commutative bialgebra. This is a structure of affine group scheme iff this bialgebra
({\mathfrak{R}},\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}},\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}})
is a Hopf algebra. Thus, a commutative bialgebra/Hopf algebra is the same as an affine monoid/group scheme (modulo direction of their morphisms).

3
Internal hom and representations

A linear representation of an algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
on a vector space
V\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
is an algebra homomorphism
\rho\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
, but from the categorical point of view the object
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
is a monoid without a structure of vector space. To require the linearity of
\rho
we need to equip
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
with a linear structure. This can be done by using the internal hom-functor. This functor and its generalised version (adjunction with a parameter) allow us to consider representations in more general monoidal categories.

3.1
Internal (co)hom-functor and its generalisation

3.1.1.

Closed categories.
A symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is called
closed
if for any
Y\in{\mathbf{C}}
the functor
-\otimes Y\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a right adjoint
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,-)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
. In this case there exists a unique bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
, which is called
(internal) hom-functor
, and an isomorphism

\displaystyle\theta=\theta_{X,Y,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X\otimes Y,Z\big)

(3.1)

natural in
X,Y,Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
(see details in
[
Bor2
, § 6.1]
or
[
MacLane
, § 4.7]
).
The natural isomorphism (
3.1
) is unique up to an automorphism of the functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(-,Y)
(or, equivalently, of the functor
-\otimes Y
).
2
2

2

If
G\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
and
G^{\prime}\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
are two right adjoints of a functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
with adjunctions
\alpha_{X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(FX,Z)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,GZ)
and
\alpha^{\prime}_{X,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(FX,Z)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,G^{\prime}Z)
, then there exists a unique natural isomorphism
\gamma_{Z}\colon GZ\to G^{\prime}Z
such that
\alpha^{\prime}_{X,Z}=(\gamma_{Z})_{*}\cdot\alpha_{X,Z}
(see the proof of
[
MacLane
, § 4.1, Cor. 1]
). In particular, by considering the case
G=G^{\prime}
we deduce that the adjunction
\alpha
is unique up to an automorphism
\gamma
.

The object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\in{\mathbf{C}}
is called
(internal) hom-object
. Due to symmetricity of
{\mathbf{C}}
the functors
-\otimes Y
and
Y\otimes-
are isomorphic, so their right adjoints are the same (up to an isomorphism of functors).

3.1.2.

Adjunction with a parameter.

The classical example of a generalisation of the notion of internal hom appears in the theory of bimodules. Let
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{C}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
. Let
M
and
N
be
({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})
- and
({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{C}})
-bimodules respectively. Their tensor product is the
({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{C}})
-module
M\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}N
. We have a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})}(M,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}(N,K))\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{C}})}(M\otimes_{\mathcal{B}}N,K),

(3.2)

where
K
is a
({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{C}})
-bimodule,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})}(-,-)
is the usual ‘external’ Hom-functor in the category of the
({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})
-bimodules and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{C}}(N,K)
is the set of right
{\mathcal{C}}
-module homomorphisms
N\to K
equipped with the structure of
({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})
-module in a natural way.

In general, let
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
be a bifunctor such that
F(-,Y)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
has a right adjoint
G_{Y}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
for any
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Then there exists a unique bifunctor
G\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
such that
G_{Y}=G(Y,-)
and the adjunction isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\big(F(X,Y),Z\big)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}\big(X,G(Y,Z)\big)

(3.3)

is natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
(see
[
MacLane
, § 4.7, Th. 3]
). This is so-called
adjunction with a parameter
with the parameter category
{\mathbf{P}}
.

3.1.3.

Generalised internal hom.

If one uses the notation
F(X,Y)=X\otimes Y
for the bifunctor
F
in (
3.3
), then we can denote
G(Y,Z)
by
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)
in a generalised sense. The adjunction with a parameter has the form (
3.1
) where
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
.

In some important cases the categories
{\mathbf{C}}
,
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
are full subcategories of a symmetric monoidal category such that
F(X,Y)=X\otimes Y\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
for any
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
and
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
. In particular, such situation takes place if
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
