for a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}
.

3.1.4.

Evaluation.

Let
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. Substitute
X=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\in{\mathbf{C}}
to (
3.1
) and take
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}
in the left hand side, then we obtain so-called
evaluation morphism

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{Y,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\otimes Y\to Z

(3.4)

in the right hand side, that is
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{Y,Z}=\theta(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)})
. This morphism is the counit of the adjunction
\big(-\otimes Y,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,-),\theta^{-1}\big)
and, in particular, it is natural in
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
; the isomorphism (
3.1
) can be expressed through the evaluation as

\displaystyle\theta_{X,Y,Z}(f)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{Y,Z}\cdot(f\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Y}),

\displaystyle f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\big),

(3.5)

(see
[
MacLane
, § 4.1]
).

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
X,\widetilde{X}\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
and
f\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)
,
g\colon\widetilde{X}\to X
be morphisms in
{\mathbf{C}}
. Then

\displaystyle\theta_{\widetilde{X},Y,Z}(f\cdot g)=\theta_{X,Y,Z}(f)\cdot(g\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Y}).

(3.6)

Proof.
By virtue of (
3.5
) we derive

\displaystyle\theta_{\widetilde{X},Y,Z}(f\cdot g)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{Y,Z}\cdot\big((f\cdot g)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Y}\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{Y,Z}\cdot(f\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Y})\cdot(g\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Y})=\theta_{X,Y,Z}(f)\cdot(g\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Y}),

where we used the functoriality of
-\otimes-\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
with respect to the first argument. ∎

3.1.5.

Internal composition.

Remind that the usual ‘external’ composition is the map
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\times\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\to\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Z)
(morphism in
{\mathbf{Set}}
). One can define the analogous morphism for the internal hom objects as a morphism in
{\mathbf{C}}
, where the role of
\times
is played by
\otimes
(see
[
Bor2
, sec. 6.1]
).

Let us consider the case of full subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. For arbitrary objects
X,Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
consider the morphism

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\otimes X\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{X,Y}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\otimes Y\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{Y,Z}}Z.

(3.7)

By applying the map

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\otimes X,Z\big)\xrightarrow{\theta^{-1}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y),\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Z)\big)

to the morphism (
3.7
) we obtain a morphism

\displaystyle c_{X,Y,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Z)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Z),

\displaystyle X,Y\in{\mathbf{P}},Z\in{\mathbf{C}}.

It is called
(internal) composition morphism
. This composition is associative in the sense that
c_{A,C,D}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes c_{A,B,C})=c_{A,B,D}\cdot(c_{B,C,D}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
for any
A,B,C\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
D\in{\mathbf{C}}
.

3.1.6.

Internal end.

Suppose again that
{\mathbf{P}}
is a full subcategory of a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes,I)={\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. By substituting
X=I
and
Z=Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
to (
3.1
) and taking the identification isomorphism
I\otimes Y=Y
in the right hand side we obtain a morphism
u_{Y}:=\theta^{-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Y})\colon I\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Y)
. The diagram

\textstyle{I\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{Y}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes u_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,Y)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{X,Y,Y}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{X,X,Y}}

commutes for any
X,Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
. In particular, for each
X\in{\mathbf{P}}
the morphisms
c_{X}:=c_{X,X,X}
and
u_{X}
give a structure of monoid on the object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,X)
. We denote this monoid by
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(X)
.

3.1.7.

Examples of closed categories.

•

In
({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
the internal hom-object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)
coincides with the ‘external’ Hom-object
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)
. The evaluation is the map
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{X,Y}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)\times X\to Y
that evaluates
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(X,Y)
on an element of
X
. Internal composition coincides with the usual composition and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(X)=\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(X)
is the usual monoid of the maps
X\to X
.

•

For the objects
V
and
W
of the monoidal category
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
the object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
is the vector space of linear maps
V\to W
. In this case it coincides with
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
as a set. The evaluation morphism
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,W}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\otimes V\to W
acts as
\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits(f\otimes v)=f(v)
,
f\colon V\to W
,
v\in V
. The composition
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,Z)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)
is given by the usual composition in the sense that it maps
f\otimes g
to
f\cdot g
. The monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
is the algebra of linear operators on
V
.

•

The monoidal category
({\mathbf{GrVect}},\otimes)
is also closed. As a vector space the internal hom in this category coincides with the internal hom in
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
, but it has additionally the structure of grading: the
k
-th component of the object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
consists of linear maps
f\colon V\to W
such that
f(V_{l})\subset W_{k+l}
. By considering the zero component of the graded space
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
as a set we can identify this component with
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
with the zero component of
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
(for this case the internal hom does not coincide with the external one as a set). The evaluation and composition look the same as for
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
. The monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
is the graded algebra of linear operators on
V
. This example is directly generalized to the case of the category of
{\mathbb{A}}
-graded vector spaces for any abelian monoid
{\mathbb{A}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{{\vphantom{M}}{}_{c}Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
.

3.1.8.

Coclosed categories.
We also need dual notions to the notions of internal hom and end. Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
be a symmetric monoidal category such that for any object
Y\in{\mathbf{C}}
the functor
-\otimes Y\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a left adjoint
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Y,-)\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
, then there is a unique bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
with a unique (up to an automorphism) natural transformation

\displaystyle\vartheta=\vartheta_{X,Y,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Y,X),Z\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,Z\otimes Y)\big).

(3.12)

A symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
satisfying this condition is called
coclosed
.
Let us call the object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Y,X)

(internal) cohom-object
. It coincides with the internal hom-object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,X)
in the opposite category
{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
coincides with the bifunctor
(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}})^{\mathrm{op}}
, it is called
(internal) cohom-functor
.

3.1.9.

Generalised cohom.

Let us consider the dual version of the adjunction with a parameter from p.
3.1
. Denote the bifunctor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\times{\mathbf{P}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
as
F(X,Y)=X\otimes Y
. If
-\otimes Y\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
has a left adjoint for any fixed
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
, then we obtain a bifunctor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathbf{C}}
with an isomorphism

\displaystyle\vartheta=\vartheta_{X,Y,Z}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Y,X),Z\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}\big(X,Z\otimes Y\big)

(3.13)

natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}
. The
coevaluation
is defined as the morphism

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{Y,X}=\vartheta(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(Y,X)})\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Y,X)\otimes Y,

(3.14)

which is natural in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
.
Then the isomorphism (
3.13
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\vartheta(f)=(f\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{Z})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{Y,X},

\displaystyle f\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Y,X)\to Z.

(3.15)

3.1.10.

Internal coend.

Suppose that
{\mathbf{P}}
is a full subcategory of a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
. By using categorical duality to the morphisms defined in p.
3.1
and
3.1
we obtain the
cocomposition

\displaystyle d_{X,Y,Z}=\vartheta^{-1}\big((\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{Z,Y})\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{Y,X}\big)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Z,X)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Y,X)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Z,Y)

(3.16)

and morphisms
v_{Y}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Y,Y)\to I
, where
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
Y,Z\in{\mathbf{P}}
. The pair
(d_{Y}:=d_{Y,Y,Y},v_{Y})
turns
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(Y,Y)
into a comonoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
. Denote it by
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(Y)
(not to confuse with ‘Coend of a functor’).

3.1.11.

Internal cohom for vector spaces.
Since the strong monoidal functor
(-)^{*}
is an equivalence between
{\mathbf{FVect}}
and
{\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, the monoidal category
({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)
is coclosed. The internal cohom-functor can be extended for the case
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FVect}}
,
{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{Vect}}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For any
W\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
the functor
-\otimes W\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
has a left adjoint
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,-)\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
, which coincides with the internal hom-functor:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(W,V)=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V),

\displaystyle V\in{\mathbf{Vect}}.

(3.17)

Proof.
Let
(w_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
,
(w^{i})_{i=1}^{m}
be dual bases of
W
and
W^{*}
, that is
w^{i}(w_{j})=\delta^{i}_{j}
. Note that for any
v\in V
and
\xi\in W^{*}
the element
\xi\otimes v\in W^{*}\otimes V
can be considered as the linear operator
W\to V
that maps
w\in W
to
\xi(w)v\in V
(in particular,
(w^{i}\otimes v)(w_{j})=\delta^{i}_{j}v
). This gives a natural isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\cong W^{*}\otimes V
. Let us define the linear operator
\eta_{V}\colon V\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\otimes W=(W^{*}\otimes V)\otimes W
by the formula
\eta_{V}(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(w^{i}\otimes v)\otimes w_{i}
. It can be also defined in the form
\eta_{V}(v)=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W}\otimes\sigma_{W,V})(1_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)}\otimes v)
, where
1_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)}=u_{W}(1)\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W,W)=W^{*}\otimes W
is the unity of the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)
, so it does not depend on the choice of the basis. Let us show that for any
V,Z\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
and a linear operator
f\colon V\to Z\otimes W
there is a unique
h\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\to Z
such that the diagram

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{V}\hskip 16.38895pt}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\otimes W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W}}
\textstyle{Z\otimes W}

commute. By decomposing the image
f
on
v\in V
we obtain
f(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}f^{i}(v)\otimes w_{i}
, where
f^{i}(v)
is the value of
f(v)\in Z\otimes W=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W^{*},Z)
on
w^{i}\in W^{*}
. The commutativity of the diagram implies that
h(w^{i}\otimes v)=f^{i}(v)=f(v)(w^{i})
, so
h
is unique. It exists due to the linearity of
f\colon V\to Z\otimes W
and
f(v)\colon W^{*}\to Z
. We obtain the universal arrow
\eta_{V}
from
V
to the functor
-\otimes W
and it is straightforward to check that
\eta_{V}
is natural in
V
. Hence the functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,-)
is a left adjoint of
-\otimes W
(see
[
MacLane
, § 4.1, Th. 2 (i)]
). ∎

3.2
(Co)representations of (co)monoids

3.2.1.

Representation of a usual monoid or a group.
Remind that for any object
V
of a category
{\mathbf{C}}
the set
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,V)
is equipped with a structure of usual monoid (monoid in
{\mathbf{Set}}
). In a wide generality we can say that a representation is a monoid homomorphism
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
. In particular, if
{\mathbb{M}}
is a group, then
\rho(m)
is an automorphism of
V
for any
m\in{\mathbb{M}}
, so we obtain a representation of a group
{\mathbb{M}}
by automorphisms of the object
V\in{\mathbf{C}}
.

If the monoids
{\mathbb{M}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
are equipped with some additional structure of the same type, one usually requires
\rho
to preserve this structure.

A morphism from a representation
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
to a representation
\rho^{\prime}\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})
is a morphism
f\colon V\to V^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{C}}
such that
f\cdot\rho(m)=\rho^{\prime}(m)\cdot f
for any
m\in{\mathbb{M}}
. In the pointless form this condition reads
f_{*}\cdot\rho=f^{*}\cdot\rho^{\prime}
.

3.2.2.

Representation of an algebra.

For example, let
V\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
; if the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
has additionally a structure of vector space compatible with the monoid structure, then both
{\mathbb{M}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
are algebras and we require
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
to be an algebra homomorphism, so we obtain the notion of ‘representation of an algebra’. More precisely, one should write there
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
instead of
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
. As we will see below this case can be generalised to an arbitrary closed symmetric monoidal category (see p.
3.2
).

Remind that the representations of a group
{\mathbb{G}}
(a group in
{\mathbf{Set}}
) or of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
can be considered as particular cases of representations of algebras, these are the representations of the corresponding group algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[{\mathbb{G}}]
or of the universal enveloping algebra
U(\mathfrak{g})
respectively. In the same way representations of a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Set}},\times)
can be identified with representations of the algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[{\mathbb{M}}]
consisting of the formal sums
\sum\limits_{m\in{\mathbb{M}}}\alpha_{m}m
,
\alpha_{m}\in{\mathbb{K}}
.

3.2.3.

Representation of an algebraic monoid/group.
Another classical example is a structure of algebraic set on a monoid/group
{\mathbb{M}}
. Suppose that this structure is compatible with the structure of monoid/group in the sense that
{\mathbb{M}}
is an algebraic monoid/group (see p.
2.5
). For
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
the monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
has a structure of algebraic monoid. In this way we obtain the representations of an algebraic monoid/group
{\mathbb{M}}
on a vector space
V
. In particular, the representation
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{n})
gives an action
a\colon{\mathbb{M}}\times{\mathbb{A}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
of a monoid/group
{\mathbb{M}}
on the object
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}\in{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
. In other words, ‘linear’ representations of a monoid/group
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{AlgSet}},\times)
is a particular case of an action of
{\mathbb{M}}
.

3.2.4.

The case of a closed category.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
be a closed symmetric monoidal category and
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
be a monoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
. Define
representation
of
{\mathbb{M}}
on an object
V\in{\mathbf{C}}
as a morphism
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
in the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. This is a morphism
\rho\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)
in
{\mathbf{C}}
such that the diagrams

\textstyle{X\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\rho\otimes\rho}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895ptc_{V}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)}

\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{u_{V}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)}

are commutative.

3.2.5.

The case of generalised internal hom.

Now consider more general case. Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
be a symmetric monoidal category and let
{\mathbf{P}}
be its full subcategory such that the functor
-\otimes Y\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a right adjoint for any
Y\in{\mathbf{P}}
, so we have the generalised
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
in the sense of p.
3.1
(the case
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}^{\prime}={\mathbf{C}}
). We get a monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
for any
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
. In this case we define
representation
of a monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
on an object
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
as a morphism
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. Again, this is a morphism
\rho\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)
making the diagrams (
3.2
) commutative.

3.2.6.

Representation as an action.
By substituting
Y=Z=V\in{\mathbf{P}}
to (
3.1
) we obtain the bijection

\displaystyle\theta\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X\otimes V,V\big).

(3.30)

Let us prove that the representations and actions on
V
are in one-to-one correspondence via this bijection.

Lemma 3.3
.

A morphism
\rho\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)
is a representation of the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
iff
a=\theta(\rho)\colon X\otimes V\to V
is an action of
{\mathbb{M}}
.

Proof.
We check that the commutativity of (
3.2
) is equivalent to the commutativity of (
2.4
). Prop.
3.1
implies
\theta(\rho\cdot\mu_{X})=\theta(\rho)\cdot(\mu_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})=a\cdot(\mu_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})\colon X\otimes X\otimes V\to V
. By virtue of the same proposition we have the equalities
\theta\big(c_{V}\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho)\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,V}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,V})\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,V}\cdot(\rho\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,V})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes\rho\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})
of morphisms
X\otimes X\otimes V\to V
. Due to (
3.5
) one can rewrite the latter one in the form
\theta\big(c_{V}\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho)\big)=a\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes a)
.
Thus
\rho\cdot\mu_{X}=c_{V}\cdot(\rho\otimes\rho)
iff
a\cdot(\mu_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})=a\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes a)
. Analogously, we derive
\theta(\rho\cdot\eta_{X})=a\cdot(\eta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})
. By taking into account
\theta(u_{V})=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}
we deduce the equivalence of the condition
\rho\cdot\eta_{X}=u_{V}
to the condition
a\cdot(\eta_{X}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}
. ∎

3.2.7.

Morphisms of representations.

Denote by
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
the category of the pairs
(V,\rho)
, where
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
\rho
is a representation of
{\mathbb{M}}
on
V
; a morphism
(V,\rho)\to(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
in this category is defined as a morphism
f\colon V\to V^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{P}}
satisfying
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V},f)\cdot\rho=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}})\cdot\rho^{\prime}
. The last equation can be written in the diagram form as

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V},f)}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V^{\prime})}

To calculate
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V},f)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}})
we will use the following general formula.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
f\colon V^{\prime}\to V
and
g\colon Z\to Z^{\prime}
be morphisms in
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}
respectively. Then

\displaystyle\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)\big)=g\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,Z}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)}\otimes f).

(3.35)

Proof.
From the naturality of
\theta_{X,V,Z}
in
V
and
Z
we obtain

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\theta}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,g)_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X\otimes V,Z\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes f)^{*}\cdot g_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X,\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},Z^{\prime})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\theta}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(X\otimes V^{\prime},Z^{\prime}\big)}

Let
X=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)
. By taking
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}
in the left upper corner of the diagram (
3.2
) we obtain the formula (
3.35
). ∎

By virtue of Lemma
3.3
we can regard the objects of the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
as objects of
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
. The definitions of morphisms in these categories coincide due to the following statement.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
a=\theta(\rho)\colon X\otimes V\to V
and
a^{\prime}=\theta(\rho)\colon X\otimes V^{\prime}\to V^{\prime}
be actions of
{\mathbb{M}}
corresponding to the representations
\rho\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
and
\rho^{\prime}\colon{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\prime})
, where
V,V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Let
f\colon V\to V^{\prime}
be a morphism in
{\mathbf{P}}
. Then the commutativity of (
3.2
) is equivalent to the commutativity of the left diagram (
2.4
).

Proof.
Application of the formulae (
3.6
) and (
3.35
) gives

\displaystyle\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}})\cdot\rho^{\prime}\big)=\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime},V^{\prime}}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},V^{\prime})}\otimes f)\cdot(\rho^{\prime}\otimes

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})=

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime},V^{\prime}}\cdot(\rho^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes f)=\theta(\rho^{\prime})\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes f)=

\displaystyle a^{\prime}\cdot(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}\otimes f),

\displaystyle\theta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V},f)\cdot\rho\big)=f\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,V}\cdot(\rho\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})=f\cdot\theta(\rho)=

\displaystyle f\cdot a,

where we also used (
3.5
). ∎

The Lemmas
3.3
and
3.5
have the following corollary.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
{\mathbf{P}}
be a full subcategory of a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
. Suppose that the functor
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
has a right adjoint for any
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Let
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. Then the morphisms (
3.30
) gives a fully faithful functor embedding the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
into
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
. In particular, if the symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is closed then we obtain an equivalence between the categories
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbb{M}})
and
\mathop{\mathbf{Lact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{M}})
.

3.2.8.

Corepresentations of comonoids.

Let us dualise the notions and results of p.
3.2
–
3.2
. Let
{\mathbf{P}}
be a full subcategory of a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
such that the functor
-\otimes V
has left adjoint for any
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
, so we have the generalised cohom-functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
with the adjunction (
3.13
). We call by
corepresentation
of a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
on
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
a morphism
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\to{\mathbb{O}}
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. This is a morphism
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\to X
in
{\mathbf{C}}
such that the diagrams

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\omega}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{X}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt\omega\otimes\omega}
\textstyle{X\otimes X}

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\omega}
\scriptstyle{v_{V}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon_{X}}
\textstyle{I}

are commutative. Denote by
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})
the category whose objects are the pairs
(V,\omega)
, where
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
\omega
is a corepresentation of
{\mathbb{O}}
on
V
, and morphisms
(V,\omega)\to(V^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
are morphisms
f\colon V\to V^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{P}}
such that

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime}},f)}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\omega^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X}

By dualising Prop.
3.4
and Theorem
3.6
we obtain the following statements.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
f\colon V^{\prime}\to V
and
g\colon Z\to Z^{\prime}
be morphisms in
{\mathbf{P}}
and
{\mathbf{C}}
respectively. Then

\displaystyle\vartheta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)\big)=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V^{\prime},Z^{\prime})}\otimes f)\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V^{\prime},Z^{\prime}}\cdot g.

(3.44)

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
. The isomorphisms

\displaystyle\vartheta_{V,V,X}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V),X\big)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(V,X\otimes V\big),

\displaystyle V\in{\mathbf{P}},

(3.45)

give a fully faithful functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})\hookrightarrow\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}})
. In other words, the morphism
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,V)\to X
is a corepresentation of
{\mathbb{O}}
iff
\delta=\vartheta(\omega)
is a coaction of
{\mathbb{O}}
on
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
, and a morphism
f\colon V\to V^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{P}}
makes the diagram (
3.2
) to be commutative for some corepresentations
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
iff it makes the right diagram (
2.4
) commutative with the coactions
\delta=\vartheta(\omega)
,
\delta^{\prime}=\vartheta(\omega^{\prime})
.

In particular, if the symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
is coclosed then we obtain an equivalence between the categories
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbb{O}})
and
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}})
.

3.3
Translation of (co)representations under monoidal functors

As we saw in p.
2.4
a (co)lax functor translates (co)actions to (co)actions. Theorems
3.6
and
3.8
imply that such functor translates the corresponding (co)representations to each other. Here we describe the translation of (co)representations explicitly.

3.3.1.

Translation of internal hom.
Let
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)
and
{\mathbf{D}}=({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be symmetric monoidal categories. Let
{\mathbf{P}}\subset{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{Q}}\subset{\mathbf{D}}
be their full subcategories such that the functors
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
and
-\odot W\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
have right adjoints for each
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
W\in{\mathbf{Q}}
, so we have generalised internal hom-functors
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
.

Let
F\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\otimes)
be a lax monoidal functor with the monoidal structure morphisms
\phi_{X,Y}\colon FX\odot FY\to F(X\otimes Y)
,
\varphi\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FI_{\mathbf{C}}
. Suppose
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
. For arbitrary
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
consider the composition

\displaystyle F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big)\odot FV\xrightarrow{\phi}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\otimes V\big)\xrightarrow{F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,Z})}FZ.

(3.46)

By applying the adjunction

\displaystyle\theta^{-1}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big)\odot FV,FZ\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big),\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FZ)\big)

(3.47)

to (
3.46
) we obtain the following morphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
:

\displaystyle\Phi_{V,Z}=\theta^{-1}\big(F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{V,Z})\cdot\phi_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z),V}\big)\colon F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FZ).

(3.48)

Thus we get a collection of morphisms
\Phi_{V,Z}
,
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
, making the diagram

\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big)\odot FV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V,Z}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FV}}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FZ)\odot FV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{FV,FZ}}
\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\otimes FV\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{VZ})}
\textstyle{FZ}

commute (the equivalence of the commutativity of (
3.3
) and the definition (
3.48
) follows from the formula (
3.5
)). The naturality of
\phi_{X,Y}
in
X\in{\mathbf{C}}
allows to prove the following properties of
\Phi_{V,Z}
.

Proposition 3.9
.

For any objects
X,Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and a morphism
f\colon X\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)
in
{\mathbf{C}}
we have the formula

\displaystyle\theta\big(\Phi_{V,Z}\cdot F(f)\big)=F\big(\theta(f)\big)\cdot\phi_{X,V}.

(3.53)

Proof.
Note that (
3.53
) is equivalent to the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{FX\odot FV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{F(f)\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FV}\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big)\odot FV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{V,Z}\odot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{FV}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FZ)\odot FV\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{FV,FZ}}
\textstyle{F(X\otimes V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(f\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V})\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\otimes FV\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F(\mathop{\mathrm{ev}}\nolimits_{VZ})}
\textstyle{FZ}

By adding the vertical arrow
\phi
to the centre we obtain two diagrams. Commutativity of the left one follows from the naturality of
\phi
, while the right one is exactly the diagram (
3.3
). ∎

Warning.
Even when
F
is strong monoidal, i.e. all
\phi_{X,Y}
and
\phi
are isomorphisms, we can not guarantee that
\Phi_{V,Z}
are also isomorphisms.

3.3.2.

Translation of the internal end.
Remind that the lax monoidal functor
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
induces the functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\colon\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
which translates each monoid
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
to the monoid
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}=(FX,\mu_{FX},\eta_{FX})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
, where
\mu_{FX}\colon FX\odot FX\to FX
and
\eta_{FX}\colon I_{\mathbf{D}}\to FX
are the compositions (
2.62
). In particular, if
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
the monoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)
is mapped to the monoid
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)
. The latter is the object
F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\big)
with the structure morphisms
F(c_{V})\cdot\phi_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V),\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)}
and
F(u_{V})\cdot\varphi
.

On the other hand, since
FV\in{\mathbf{Q}}
we have the monoid

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FV),c_{FV},u_{FV}\big)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}}).

(3.58)

We get a relationship between these two monoids by means of the morphisms (
3.48
).

Proposition 3.10
.

For any
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
the morphism
\Phi_{V,V}\colon F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\big)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FV)
in
{\mathbf{D}}
gives a morphism
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
. In particular, if
\Phi_{V,V}
is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
then the monoids
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\big)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)
are isomorphic to each other (as objects of
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
).

Proof.
The first sentence of the proposition will be proved in p.
3.3
in more general settings (see Remark
3.12
). The second sentence follows from the first one and Prop.
2.1
. ∎

3.3.3.

Translation of representations.

Since
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
, the restriction of the functor (
2.93
) gives the functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}),

(3.59)

where
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}
. Let us describe it explicitly.

Proposition 3.11
.

The functor (
3.59
) maps an object
(V,\rho)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{M}})
to
(FV,\widetilde{\rho})
, where
\widetilde{\rho}\colon\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)
is the composition

\displaystyle FX\xrightarrow{F\rho}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)\big)\xrightarrow{\Phi_{V,V}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(FV,FV).

(3.60)

Proof.
Let
a=\theta(\rho)
. The functor (
2.93
) maps
(V,a)
to
(FV,\widetilde{a})
, where
\widetilde{a}=F(a)\cdot\phi_{X,V}
. By applying Prop.
3.9
to
f=\rho
we derive
\theta(\Phi_{V,V}\cdot F\rho)=F\big(\theta(\rho)\big)\cdot\phi=F(a)\cdot\phi=\widetilde{a}
, so the action
\widetilde{a}
corresponds to the representation (
3.60
). ∎

Remark 3.12
.

The fact that (
3.60
) is a morphism of monoids
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(FV)
follows immediately from Prop.
3.10
and functoriality of
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F)
. Conversely, by applying Prop.
3.11
to the case
X=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(V,V)
,
\rho=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{X}
and by taking into account the statements written in p.
2.4
we see that
\Phi_{V,V}
is a representation. This implies the first sentence of Prop.
3.10
.

3.3.4.

Translation of internal cohom, coend and of corepresentations.
In the same setting suppose that the functors
-\otimes V\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
and
-\odot W\colon{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
have left adjoints for each
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
W\in{\mathbf{Q}}
instead of right adjoints, so we have generalised internal cohom-functors
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{D}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
.

Let
F=(F,\varphi,\phi)\colon({\mathbf{C}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{D}},\odot)
be a colax monoidal functor such that
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
. For arbitrary
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
consider the composition

\displaystyle FZ\xrightarrow{F(\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,Z})}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\otimes V\big)\xrightarrow{\phi}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big)\odot FV.

(3.61)

By applying the adjunction we obtain

\displaystyle\Phi_{V,Z}=\vartheta^{-1}\big(\phi_{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,Z),V}\cdot F(\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits_{V,Z})\big)\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(FV,FZ)\to F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,Z)\big),

(3.62)

where
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
,
Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
. These morphisms satisfies

\displaystyle\vartheta\big(F(f)\cdot\Phi_{V,Z}\big)=\phi_{X,V}\cdot F\big(\vartheta(f)\big)

(3.63)

for any
X,Z\in{\mathbf{C}}
,
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{C}}\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(V,Z),X\big)
.

Proposition 3.13
.

For any
V\in{\mathbf{P}}
the morphism
\Phi_{V,V}
gives a morphism of comonoids

\displaystyle\Phi_{V,V}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(FV)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F)\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\big).

(3.64)

In particular, if
\Phi_{V,V}
is an isomorphism in
{\mathbf{D}}
, then these monoids are isomorphic.

Let
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{O}}
. Then the restriction of the functor (
2.94
) is the functor

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{O}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}).

(3.65)

It maps the object
(V,\omega)
to
(FV,\widetilde{\omega})
, where
\widetilde{\omega}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(FV)\to\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
is a corepresentation of
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}
on
FV
given by the composition

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(FV)\xrightarrow{\Phi_{V,V}}F\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(V)\big)\xrightarrow{F\omega}FX.

(3.66)

3.3.5.

Translation of monoidal product of (co)representations.

Let
F\colon{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a symmetric strong monoidal functor such that
F({\mathbf{P}})\subset{\mathbf{Q}}
. Let
{\mathbb{B}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X},\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})
be a bimonoid in
{\mathbf{C}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{B}}
be the corresponding bimonoid in
{\mathbf{D}}
. Then the restriction of the functors (
2.95
) are the functors (
3.59
) and (
3.65
), where we have
{\mathbb{M}}=(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
and
{\mathbb{O}}=(X,\Delta_{X},\varepsilon_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{C}})
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{O}}=\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits(F){\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{D}})
. Since the functors (
2.95
) are symmetric strong monoidal, the restriction gives the symmetric strong monoidal functors

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}),

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}).

(3.67)

For example, let
\rho
and
\pi
be representations of the monoid
{\mathbb{M}}
on
W\in{\mathbf{P}}
and
Z\in{\mathbf{P}}
. Their monoidal product in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{P}}({\mathbb{B}})
is a representation
\tau
on
W\otimes Z
. The functor (
3.59
) maps them to representations
\widetilde{\rho}
,
\widetilde{\pi}
and
\widetilde{\tau}
on
FW
,
FZ
and
F(W\otimes Z)
respectively. The monoidal product of
\widetilde{\rho}
and
\widetilde{\pi}
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}})
is a representation
\lambda
on
FW\odot FZ
. The isomorphism
\phi_{W,Z}\colon FW\odot FZ\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}F(W\otimes Z)
gives the isomorphism
\big(FW\odot FZ,\lambda\big)\xrightarrow{\smash{\raisebox{-1.959pt}{$\sim$}}}\big(F(W\otimes Z),\widetilde{\tau}\big)
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Q}}(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}})
. In the same way one can describe the isomorphism of the corresponding corepresentations.

Remark 3.14
.

The strong monoidality of the functors (
3.67
) follows from the strong monoidality of the functors (
2.95
). Alternatively, one can prove this in a direct way by using the formula (
3.35
) and Prop.
2.1
.

4
Quantum linear spaces

Idea.
Consider the sets
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
and
{\mathbb{K}}^{m}
as vector (linear) spaces, that is as objects of
{\mathbf{Vect}}
or
{\mathbf{FVect}}
. The morphisms between these vector spaces are linear maps
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{K}}^{m}
(matrices
m\times n
over
{\mathbb{K}}
). If we (partially) forget linear structure of these spaces we obtain the affine spaces
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}
and
{\mathbb{A}}^{m}
, which are the objects of
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}\subset{\mathbf{AffSch}}
. Since
{\mathbf{AffSch}}={\mathbf{CommAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
A({\mathbb{A}}^{n})={\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
, the morphisms
{\mathbb{A}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{m}
is in one-to-one correspondence with the algebra homomorphisms
f\colon{\mathbb{K}}[y^{1},\ldots,y^{m}]\to{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
. Namely, if a homomorphism
f
is given by the images
f(y^{i})=P_{i}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})\in{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
for some polynomials
P_{i}
, then the corresponding morphism
\Phi\colon{\mathbb{A}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{m}
is the map
\Phi(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})=\big(P_{1}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}),\ldots,P_{m}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})\big)
. Now let us recall back the linear structure of
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}={\mathbb{A}}^{n}
and
{\mathbb{K}}^{m}={\mathbb{A}}^{m}
. The map
\Phi
preserves the linear structure iff all
P_{i}
are homogeneous polynomials of order
1
, that is
P_{i}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{ij}x^{j}
for some
a_{ij}\in{\mathbb{K}}
. This means exactly that the homomorphism
f
preserves the grading of the algebras
{\mathbb{K}}[y^{1},\ldots,y^{m}]
and
{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
(the
k
-th component of
{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
is the space of all the homogeneous polynomials of order
k
). Any linear map
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{K}}^{m}
is arisen in this way: it is given by the
m\times n
matrix
(a_{ij})
.

Hence a natural candidates to a quantum analogue of a vector spaces are graded algebras or at least some of them, considered as objects of (a subcategory of) the category
{\mathbf{GrAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. In
[
Man87
,
Man88
]
Yuri Manin proposed a subcategory
{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
to generalise the finite-dimensional vector spaces. He introduced the notion ‘
quantum linear space
’ for this case.

4.1
Operations with quadratic algebras

4.1.1.

Manin’s binary operations.

Let
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
be arbitrary quadratic algebras (here we do not suppose that the quadratic algebras are finitely generated). They can be presented in the form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}=TW/(S),

(4.1)

where
V,W\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
and
(R)\subset TV
,
(S)\subset TW
are ideals generated by subspaces
R\subset V^{\otimes 2}
,
S\subset W^{\otimes 2}
respectively. Define the following operations
[
Man87
,
Man88
]
.

•

Manin white product:

{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}=T(V\otimes W)/(R_{\mathrm{w}})
, where

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{w}}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\otimes\sigma_{V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})(R\otimes W\otimes W+V\otimes V\otimes S)\subset V\otimes W\otimes V\otimes W.

•

Manin black product:

{\mathcal{A}}\bullet{\mathcal{B}}=T(V\otimes W)/(R_{\mathrm{b}})
, where

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{b}}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\otimes\sigma_{V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})(R\otimes S)\subset V\otimes W\otimes V\otimes W.

•

(Even) tensor product:

{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}=T(V\oplus W)/\big(R\oplus[V,W]\oplus S\big)
, where

[V,W]=\{v\otimes w-w\otimes v\mid v\in V,w\in W\}\subset(V\otimes W)\oplus(W\otimes V)\subset(V\oplus W)^{\otimes 2}.

•

Odd tensor product:

{\mathcal{A}}\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}=T(V\oplus W)/\big(R\oplus[V,W]_{+}\oplus S\big)
, where

[V,W]_{+}=\{v\otimes w+w\otimes v\mid v\in V,w\in W\}\subset(V\otimes W)\oplus(W\otimes V)\subset(V\oplus W)^{\otimes 2}.

4.1.2.

Properties of Manin’s binary operations

[
Man88
]
. The Manin white product and the even tensor product coincide with the Manin product
{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
and the usual tensor product
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
defined in p.
2.3
for general graded algebras. All four operations are bifunctors
{\mathbf{QA}}\times{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}
equipping
{\mathbf{QA}}
with monoidal structures. The unit objects of
({\mathbf{QA}},\circ)
and
({\mathbf{QA}},\bullet)
are
{\mathbb{K}}[u]
and
{\mathbb{K}}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2})
respectively. The unit object of
({\mathbf{QA}},\otimes)
and
({\mathbf{QA}},\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}})
is
{\mathbb{K}}
. The subcategory
{\mathbf{FQA}}\subset{\mathbf{QA}}
inherits all four monoidal structures. The inclusion
R_{\mathrm{b}}\subset R_{\mathrm{w}}
defines an epimorphism
{\mathcal{A}}\bullet{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
.

4.1.3.

The functor
(-)_{1}
.

If we have a graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
we can take its first graded component
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
. In this way we obtain the functor
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{GrAlg}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
, which maps a graded homomorphism
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{GrAlg}}}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}})
to its first order component
f_{1}\colon{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
. Restriction gives functors
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
and
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}\to{\mathbf{FVect}}
, which translate a quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)
to the vector space
V
. If
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
, then a graded homomorphism
f\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
can be uniquely restored by the component
f_{1}
. Hence the functors
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
and
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}\to{\mathbf{FVect}}
are faithful. By applying these functors to the result of the Manin’s binary operations we obtain the following natural isomorphisms:

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}})_{1}=({\mathcal{A}}\bullet{\mathcal{B}})_{1}={\mathcal{A}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}_{1},

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}})_{1}=({\mathcal{A}}\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}})_{1}={\mathcal{A}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{B}}_{1}.

(4.2)

This means that the functors
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
and
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}\to{\mathbf{FVect}}
are equipped with the following strong monoidal structures

\displaystyle({\mathbf{QA}},\circ)\to({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes),

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FQA}},\circ)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes),

(4.3)

\displaystyle({\mathbf{QA}},\bullet)\to({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes),

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FQA}},\bullet)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes),

(4.4)

\displaystyle({\mathbf{QA}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus),

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FQA}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus),

(4.5)

\displaystyle({\mathbf{QA}},\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}})\to({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus),

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FQA}},\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}})\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus).

(4.6)

The fullness of the functor
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
and the left isomorphism (
4.2
) imply that the epimorphism
{\mathcal{A}}\bullet{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
is natural in
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
.

Note also that the functors
T\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}
and
T\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}
considered in p.
2.3
are left adjoints of
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
and
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}\to{\mathbf{FVect}}
respectively.

We see that the binary operations
\circ
and
\bullet
are related with the tensor product of vector spaces, while
\otimes
and
\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}
are related with the direct sum
\oplus
. Below we introduce two embedding of
{\mathbf{FVect}}
into
{\mathbf{FQA}}^{{\mathrm{op}}}
: ‘even’ and ‘odd’. The former one will relate the monoidal products
\otimes
and
\oplus
on
{\mathbf{FVect}}
to
\circ
and
\otimes
on
{\mathbf{FQA}}
, while the odd embedding will relate
\otimes
and
\oplus
to
\bullet
and
\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}
respectively.

4.1.4.

Purely even/odd quadratic super-algebras.

Let
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}={\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}=\{\bar{0},\bar{1}\}
. A
quadratic super-algebra
over
{\mathbb{K}}
is a quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)
with the structure of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-grading compatible with multiplication and with
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
-grading in the sense that each component
{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
is a direct sum of its subspaces of even and odd elements:
{\mathcal{A}}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}}_{k}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}({\mathcal{A}}_{k})_{\bar{0}}\oplus({\mathcal{A}}_{k})_{\bar{1}}
. Such algebra is generated by (basis) elements of the subspaces
V_{\bar{0}}=(A_{1})_{\bar{0}}
and
V_{\bar{1}}=(A_{1})_{\bar{1}}
.
We call it
commutative
if
ab=-ba
for
a,b\in V_{\bar{1}}
and
ab=ba
for
a\in V_{\bar{0}}
,
b\in V
(this is exactly the commutativity defined in p.
2.4
for the monoidal category of super-vector spaces
[
Del
, § 1.1, 1.2]
). We call a quadratic super-algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)

purely even
or
purely odd
if
V=V_{\bar{0}}
or
V=V_{\bar{1}}
respectively. The commutative purely even and odd quadratic super-algebras over
{\mathbb{K}}
form categories, which we denote by
{\mathbf{CommQSA}}_{\mathrm{e}ven}
and
{\mathbf{CommQSA}}_{\mathrm{o}dd}
. They can be identified with subcategories of
{\mathbf{QA}}
consisting of commutative
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
(i.e.
ab=ba

\;\forall\,a,b\in{\mathcal{A}}
) and of
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
satisfying
a_{1}b_{1}=-b_{1}a_{1}

\;\forall\,a_{1},b_{1}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
respectively. The additional condition that they are finitely generated gives the subcategories

\displaystyle{\mathbf{FCommQSA}}_{\mathrm{e}ven}={\mathbf{CommQSA}}_{\mathrm{e}ven}\cap{\mathbf{FQA}},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{FCommQSA}}_{\mathrm{o}dd}={\mathbf{CommQSA}}_{\mathrm{o}dd}\cap{\mathbf{FQA}}.

4.1.5.

The functors
S
and
\Lambda
.

For a vector space
V\in{\mathbf{Vect}}

symmetric algebra
and
exterior algebra
are the quadratic algebras

\displaystyle SV=TV/\big(\{v_{1}\otimes v_{2}-v_{2}\otimes v_{1}\mid v_{1},v_{2}\in V\}\big),

(4.7)

\displaystyle\Lambda V=TV/\big(\{v_{1}\otimes v_{2}+v_{2}\otimes v_{1}\mid v_{1},v_{2}\in V\}\big).

(4.8)

We obtain the functors
S\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}
and
\Lambda\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}
and their ‘finite-dimensional’ versions
S\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}
and
\Lambda\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}
. The functors
S
and
\Lambda
are left adjoint functors to the restrictions of the functor
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
to the subcategories
{\mathbf{CommQSA}}_{\mathrm{e}ven}
and
{\mathbf{CommQSA}}_{\mathrm{o}dd}
respectively. Analogously, the finite-dimensional versions are left adjoints to the restrictions of
(-)_{1}\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}\to{\mathbf{FVect}}
to the subcategories
{\mathbf{FCommQSA}}_{\mathrm{e}ven}
and
{\mathbf{FCommQSA}}_{\mathrm{o}dd}
.

Denote by
S^{k}V
and
\Lambda^{k}V
the
k
-th graded components of
SV
and
\Lambda V
. In these notations we can write the following natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle S^{k}(V\oplus W)\cong\bigoplus_{l=0}^{k}(S^{l}V)\otimes(S^{k-l}W),

\displaystyle\Lambda^{k}(V\oplus W)\cong\bigoplus_{l=0}^{k}(\Lambda^{l}V)\otimes(\Lambda^{k-l}W)

(4.9)

in
{\mathbf{Vect}}
. Summation over
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
gives the natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle S(V\oplus W)\cong(SV)\otimes(SW),

\displaystyle\Lambda(V\oplus W)\cong(\Lambda V)\otimes(\Lambda W)

(4.10)

in
{\mathbf{QA}}
.
They are the structure morphisms of the
strong
monoidal functors

\displaystyle S\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{QA}},\otimes),

\displaystyle S\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{FQA}},\otimes),

(4.11)

\displaystyle\Lambda\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{QA}},\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}),

\displaystyle\Lambda\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{FQA}},\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}).

(4.12)

On the other hand the natural transformations

\displaystyle S(V\otimes W)\to(SV)\circ(SW),

\displaystyle(\Lambda V)\bullet(\Lambda W)\to\Lambda(V\otimes W)

(4.13)

are not isomorphisms even for finite-dimensional
V
and
W
. This does not give strong monoidal functors. Nevertheless, we obtain
colax
monoidal functors

\displaystyle S\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{QA}},\circ),

\displaystyle S\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}},\circ)

(4.14)

and
lax
monoidal functors

\displaystyle\Lambda\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{QA}},\bullet),

\displaystyle\Lambda\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}},\bullet).

(4.15)

The functors
S
and
\Lambda
are faithful since their compositions with the functor
(-)_{1}
coincide with the identical functor:
(SV)_{1}=V
,
(\Lambda V)_{1}=V
. Moreover, any morphism
SV\to SW
or
\Lambda V\to\Lambda W
is uniquely given by its first component and hence have the form
Sf
or, respectively,
\Lambda f
for some linear map
f\colon V\to W
, so that the functors
S
and
\Lambda
are fully faithful.

4.1.6.

Koszul duality for quadratic algebras

[
Man87
,
Man88
]
. Consider a finitely generated quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)
, where
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
and
R\subset V^{\otimes 2}
. Define its
Koszul dual quadratic algebra
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{!}=T(V^{*})/(R^{\bot}),

\displaystyle R^{\bot}=\{\xi\in V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\mid\xi(r)=0\;\forall\,r\in R\}.

(4.16)

The operation (
4.16
) is a contravariant functor
(-)^{!}\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}
. We have the following natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}^{!})^{!}\cong{\mathcal{A}},

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}})^{!}\cong{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\bullet{\mathcal{B}}^{!},

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}\bullet{\mathcal{B}})^{!}\cong{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\circ{\mathcal{B}}^{!},

(4.17)

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}^{!})_{1}=({\mathcal{A}}_{1})^{*},

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}})^{!}\cong{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}^{!},

\displaystyle({\mathcal{A}}\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}})^{!}\cong{\mathcal{A}}^{!}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}^{!}.

(4.18)

We see that this functor is an involutive anti-autoequivalence, which interchanges the Manin’s operations, and that the diagrams

\textstyle{{\mathbf{FQA}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)^{!}}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{FVect}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}}}

\textstyle{{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)^{!}}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{FQA}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{FVect}}

are commutative.

4.1.7.

The functors
S^{*}
and
\Lambda^{*}
.
Define contravariant functors
S^{*}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}
and
\Lambda^{*}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}
by the formulae
S^{*}(V)=S(V^{*})
,
\Lambda^{*}(V)=\Lambda(V^{*})
,
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
. As functors
{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
they are the compositions of
(-)^{*}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}}
with the functors
S^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
\Lambda^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
respectively, which are the opposite functors to ones defined in p.
4.1
.

The algebra
S^{*}(V)
is the algebra of polynomial functions on
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
. By choosing a basis
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
in
V
we obtain an isomorphism
V\cong{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
and hence
S^{*}(V)\cong S^{*}({\mathbb{K}}^{n})={\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
, where
(x^{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is the dual basis of
V^{*}\cong({\mathbb{K}}^{n})^{*}
.

As a fully faithful functor the functor
S^{*}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
embeds the finite-dimensional vector spaces into the category
{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. This justifies the term ‘quantum linear spaces’ for the objects of
{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, but this is only finite-dimensional quantum analogue.

The functor
\Lambda^{*}
also embeds
{\mathbf{FVect}}
into
{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, but in a different way. For
V={\mathbb{K}}^{n}
the algebra
\Lambda^{*}({\mathbb{K}}^{n})
is isomorphic to the Grassmann algebra with generators
\psi^{1},\ldots,\psi^{n}
and relations
\psi^{i}\psi^{j}+\psi^{j}\psi^{i}=0
.

Thus, the functors
S^{*}
and
\Lambda^{*}
are two different embeddings of the category
{\mathbf{FVect}}
into
{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. They identify the category
{\mathbf{FVect}}
with some full subcategories of the categories
{\mathbf{FCommQSA}}_{\mathrm{e}ven}^{\mathrm{op}}\subset{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
{\mathbf{FCommQSA}}_{\mathrm{o}dd}^{\mathrm{op}}\subset{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
respectively. Hence the embeddings
S^{*}
and
\Lambda^{*}
can be interpreted as follows. Any vector space
V
can be considered as a purely even or as a purely odd super-vector space. In the former case we apply
S^{*}
to consider the vector space as quantum linear vector space
S^{*}V=S(V^{*})
, in the latter case we apply
\Lambda^{*}
.

Note that
S^{*}(V)=S(V^{*})=(\Lambda V)^{!}
and
\Lambda^{*}(V)=\Lambda(V^{*})=(SV)^{!}
. This means that the functor
(-)^{!}
maps a vector space to its dual with change of its parity. The operations
{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
are analogues of tensor product and direct sum of vector spaces for the case of the quantum linear spaces
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
considered as purely even. The operations
{\mathcal{A}}\bullet{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}}{\mathcal{B}}
are also analogues of the tensor product and direct sum, but in this case we need to consider
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
as purely odd.

4.1.8.

Monoidal structures of
S^{*}
.

Let us chose the ‘even’ interpretation: consider
{\mathbf{FVect}}
as the subcategory of
{\mathbf{CommQSA}}_{\mathrm{e}ven}^{\mathrm{op}}\subset{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
by means of
S^{*}
. Since the monoidal functor (
4.11
) can be regarded as strict monoidal, the operation
\oplus
in
{\mathbf{FVect}}
coincides with the operation
\otimes
in
{\mathbf{FQA}}
(see the formula (
4.10
)). On the other hand, the functor (
4.14
) is colax monoidal, so its opposite
S^{\mathrm{op}}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
is lax monoidal. By composing the latter one with the strong monoidal functor
(-)^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
in a proper order we obtain a lax monoidal functor. Thus we have

strong
monoidal functor

\displaystyle S^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes),

(4.27)

lax
monoidal functor

\displaystyle S^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ).

(4.28)

In particular, the tensor product of vector spaces does not coincide with the result of the Manin white product after the embedding
S^{*}
, because the monoidal functor (
4.28
) is not strong monoidal.

Remark 4.1
.

One can define a functor
S^{*}\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
as the composition of the functors
(-)^{*}\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
S^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. It has a structure of strong monoidal functor
({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
, but it does not get any monoidal structure
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
, since
(-)^{*}\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)
is colax monoidal, while
S^{\mathrm{op}}\colon({\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
is lax monoidal.

4.1.9.

Coproduct of quadratic algebras.

The product of quantum linear spaces is the coproduct in the category
{\mathbf{FQA}}
or, more generally, in
{\mathbf{QA}}
. For any two quadratic algebras
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)\in{\mathbf{QA}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}=TV^{\prime}/(R^{\prime})\in{\mathbf{QA}}
their coproduct exists and has the form

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}\amalg{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}:=T(V\oplus V^{\prime})/(R\oplus R^{\prime}).

(4.29)

Indeed, let
{\mathcal{B}}=TW/(S)\in{\mathbf{QA}}
and
f\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
,
f^{\prime}\colon{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{B}}
be two morphisms in
{\mathbf{QA}}
. Their first order components satisfy
(f_{1}\otimes f_{1})R\subset S
,
(f^{\prime}_{1}\otimes f^{\prime}_{1})R^{\prime}\subset S
. There exists a unique linear map
h_{1}\colon V\oplus V^{\prime}\to W
such that the diagram

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{V\oplus V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}}
\textstyle{V^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{W}

commutes. Since
(h_{1}\otimes h_{1})(R\oplus R^{\prime})=(f_{1}\otimes f_{1})(R)+(f^{\prime}_{1}\otimes f^{\prime}_{1})(R^{\prime})\subset S
, there exists a morphism
h\colon{\mathcal{A}}\amalg{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{B}}
with the first order component
h_{1}
. Hence there is a unique morphism
h
making the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\amalg{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

commutative.

Note that the inclusion
R\oplus R^{\prime}\subset R\oplus[V,V^{\prime}]\oplus R^{\prime}
implies the natural epimorphism

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}\amalg{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}.

(4.38)

Remark 4.2
.

The coproduct in the category the connected affinely generated graded algebras is different. Namely, the coproduct of
{\mathcal{A}}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{k}
in this category is the graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\coprod\limits_{c.a.g.}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
with the components

\displaystyle\Big({\mathcal{A}}\coprod\limits_{c.a.g.}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\Big)_{k}=\bigoplus_{p=1}^{k+1}\bigoplus_{l_{1}=0}^{k}\bigoplus_{l_{2},\ldots,l_{p}=1\atop l_{1}+l_{2}+\ldots+l_{p}=k}^{k}({\mathcal{A}}_{l_{1}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{l_{2}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}_{l_{3}}\otimes\cdots).

(4.39)

4.1.10.

Functor
T^{*}
.

To extend the tensor product of vector spaces in an exact way one can consider another embedding such as the fully faithful functor
T^{*}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
defined as the composition of
(-)^{*}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}\to{\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
T^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{FVect}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, that is
T^{*}(V)=T(V^{*})
. It is a strong monoidal functor
T^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
, but in this case the direct sum
\oplus
is not strong-monoidally related with the Manin’s operation
\otimes
with quadratic algebras. Indeed,
T(V)\otimes T(W)
is a quotient algebra of
T(V\oplus W)
over the ideal generated by the subset
\{v\otimes w-w\otimes v\mid v\in V,\,w\in W\}
, so
T\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{FQA}},\otimes)
is a colax (not strong) monoidal functor.

The functor
T
preserves the finite coproducts, hence it has a structure of strong monoidal functor
({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{QA}},\amalg)
. If
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
then
{\mathcal{A}}\amalg{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
, so we also obtain the strong monoidal functor
T\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{FQA}},\amalg)
.
Thus we have:

lax
monoidal functor

\displaystyle T^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes),

(4.40)

strong
monoidal functor

\displaystyle T^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\amalg),

(4.41)

strong
monoidal functor

\displaystyle T^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ),

(4.42)

strong
monoidal functor

\displaystyle T^{*}\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\oplus)\to({\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\amalg),

(4.43)

colax
monoidal functor

\displaystyle T^{*}\colon({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ),

(4.44)

where
T^{*}\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the composition of the functors
(-)^{*}\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}}
and
T^{\mathrm{op}}\colon{\mathbf{Vect}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

4.1.11.

Dequantisation functor.
Composition of the functors
(-)^{*}
and
(-)_{1}
gives the functor
(-)^{*}_{1}\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{FVect}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}\mapsto({\mathcal{A}})_{1}^{*}=({\mathcal{A}}_{1})^{*}
. By composing it with any of the functors
S^{*}
,
\Lambda^{*}
and
T^{*}
, which embed
{\mathbf{FVect}}
into
{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, we obtain the identical functor:

\displaystyle\big(S^{*}(V)\big)_{1}^{*}=V,

\displaystyle\big(\Lambda^{*}(V)\big)_{1}^{*}=V,

\displaystyle\big(T^{*}(V)\big)_{1}^{*}=V.

(4.45)

The functor
(-)_{1}^{*}\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{FVect}}
is faithful, but not full. It has the following structures of
strong
monoidal functor:

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus),

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes),

(4.46)

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbin{\underline{\otimes}})\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus),

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\bullet)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes),

(4.47)

\displaystyle({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\amalg)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\oplus).

(4.48)

This functor extracts the classical part from a quadratic algebra. In other words, this is a classical limit for quantum linear spaces.

It can be extended to the functor
(-)^{*}_{1}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Vect}}
, which has a lax monoidal structure
(-)_{1}^{*}\colon({\mathbf{QA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)\to({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
, but the identities (
4.45
) do not hold for infinite-dimensional vector spaces
V
.

4.2
Manin matrices

To define Manin matrices in terms of matrix idempotents we first describe the relationship between quadratic algebras and general idempotent operators.

4.2.1.

Quadratic algebras for idempotents.

Remind that an
idempotent
is an element
A
of a ring
{\mathfrak{R}}
satisfying
A^{2}=A
. Let
W
be a vector space and
R,R^{\prime}\subset W
be its subspaces such that
W=R\oplus R^{\prime}
. The composition
W\twoheadrightarrow W/R^{\prime}\cong R\hookrightarrow W
is an idempotent in the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)
. Moreover, the idempotents
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)
are in one-to-one correspondence with the decompositions
W=R\oplus R^{\prime}
into subspaces
R,R^{\prime}\subset W
such that
R=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits A
and
R^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(1-A)
, where
1=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V\otimes V}
. The operator
1-A\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)
is the
dual
idempotent, it corresponds to the decomposition
W=R^{\prime}\oplus R
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
have the form
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)
for a vector space
V\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
and a subspace
R\subset V^{\otimes 2}
. As any subspace the subspace
R
can be given as an image of an idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\otimes 2})
, since
R
always has a complement
R^{\prime}\subset V^{\otimes 2}
. Thus any connected quadratic algebra is isomorphic to
TV/(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits A)
for some idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\otimes 2})
.

Denote
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}})=TV/(R^{\prime})
where
R^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(1-A)
. Any
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
is isomorphic to
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
for some idempotent operator
A
. More generally, any semi-connected quadratic algebra is isomorphic to
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}}):={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
for some
A
and
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
.

If
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
then
(V^{\otimes 2})^{*}=(V^{*})^{\otimes 2}
, so any (idempotent) operator
A\colon V^{\otimes 2}\to V^{\otimes 2}
gives the (idempotent) transpose operator
A^{*}\colon(V^{*})^{\otimes 2}\to(V^{*})^{\otimes 2}
. Any quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
or
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
isomorphic to the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}}):=TV^{*}/(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits A^{*})=\Xi_{1-A^{*}}({\mathbb{K}})
or
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}}):={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})=\Xi_{1-A^{*}}({\mathfrak{R}})
respectively for some idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\otimes 2})
. The Koszul duality in these notations has the form:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})^{!}=\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}}),

\displaystyle\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}})^{!}={\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}}).

(4.49)

The semi-connected quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
can be considered as values of functors
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A},\Xi_{A}\colon{\mathbf{Alg}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
coinciding with
-\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
and
-\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{!}
, where
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
.

For the idempotent
A_{V}=\dfrac{1-\sigma_{V,V}}{2}
(anti-symmetrizer of
V\otimes V
) we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{X}}_{A_{V}}({\mathbb{K}})=S^{*}(V),

\displaystyle\Xi_{A_{V}}({\mathbb{K}})=\Lambda V,

(4.50)

The functors
T^{*}
and
T
give the quadratic algebras for the trivial idempotents
0,1\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\otimes 2})
:

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{X}}_{0}({\mathbb{K}})=T^{*}(V),

\displaystyle\Xi_{1}({\mathbb{K}})=TV.

(4.51)

4.2.2.

The case of matrix idempotents.

A basis
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
of a vector space
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
gives the isomorphism
V\cong{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
,
v\mapsto(x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
, where
x^{i}
are components of
v\in V
defined by the formula
v=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{i}v_{i}
. It induces the algebra isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{n})
, which maps operator on
V
to its
n\times n
matrix in the basis
(v_{i})
. The space
V\otimes V
has the basis
(v_{i}\otimes v_{j})_{i,j=1}^{n}
, so the operators on
V\otimes V
correspond to the
n^{2}\times n^{2}
matrices, whose entries have two pairs of indices. Namely, the operator
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\otimes 2})
corresponds to the matrix
A=(A^{ij}_{kl})
defined by the formula
A(v_{k}\otimes v_{l})=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}A^{ij}_{kl}v_{i}\otimes v_{j}
. The transpose operator
A^{*}\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{*}\otimes V^{*})
has the form
A^{*}(v^{i}\otimes v^{j})=\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}A^{ij}_{kl}v^{k}\otimes v^{l}
, where
(v^{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is the dual basis.

Since any
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
has a finite basis
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, any algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
is isomorphic to
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
for some matrix idempotent
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{n})
. The latter is a matrix
A=(A^{ij}_{kl})
such that
\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}A^{ij}_{kl}A^{kl}_{ab}=A^{ij}_{ab}
. The algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
is generated by
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}
with the quadratic commutation relations
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}A^{ij}_{kl}v_{j}v_{j}=v_{k}v_{l}
, where we identified the basis
(v_{i})
with the standard basis of
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
.

The same algebra is isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})=TW/(R)
for another matrix idempotent
A
such that
W=({\mathbb{K}}^{n})^{*}
and
R=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits A^{*}
. Let
(v^{i})
be the basis of
({\mathbb{K}}^{n})^{*}
dual to the standard basis of
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
, then
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
is generated by
v^{1},\ldots,v^{n}
with the relations
\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}A^{ij}_{kl}v^{k}v^{l}=0
.

Consider, for instance, the anti-symmetrizer
A_{n}=A_{{\mathbb{K}}^{n}}\colon{\mathbb{K}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{K}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
. It has the entries
(A_{n})^{ij}_{kl}=\frac{1}{2}(\delta^{i}_{k}\delta^{j}_{l}-\delta^{i}_{l}\delta^{j}_{k})
