. The corresponding quadratic algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
and
\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
are generated by
v^{i}
and
v_{i}
with the relations
v^{i}v^{j}=v^{j}v^{i}
and
v_{i}v_{j}=-v_{j}v_{i}
. The former one is the polynomial algebra
{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]
with commutative variables
x^{i}=v^{i}
, while the latter one is the Grassmann algebra with the anti-commutative variables
v_{i}
.

4.2.3.

Manin matrix for a pair of idempotents.

Any
n\times m
matrix
M
with entries
M_{ij}=M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
can be considered as an operator
{\mathbb{K}}^{m}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
. In terms of standard bases
(v_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and
(w_{j})_{j=1}^{m}
of the spaces
{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
and
{\mathbb{K}}^{m}
it acts as
Mw_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M^{i}_{j}\otimes v_{i}
. Denote by
M^{(a)}
the operator
M
acting on the
a
-th matrix tensor factor
{\mathbb{K}}^{m}
of
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{m}\otimes\cdots\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{m}
. In the case of two such factors we have

\displaystyle M^{(1)}(r\otimes w_{j}\otimes w_{l})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}M^{i}_{j}r\otimes v_{i}\otimes w_{l},

\displaystyle r\in{\mathfrak{R}},

(4.52)

\displaystyle M^{(2)}(r\otimes w_{j}\otimes w_{l})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}M^{k}_{l}r\otimes w_{j}\otimes v_{k},

\displaystyle r\in{\mathfrak{R}}.

(4.53)

Definition 4.3
.

[
S
]

Let
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{n})
and
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{m})
be matrix idempotents. The matrix
M
satisfying

\displaystyle AM^{(1)}M^{(2)}(1-B)=0

(4.54)

is called
Manin matrix for the pair of idempotents
(A,B)
or simply
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
.
In the case
A=B
we will say
B
-Manin matrix
instead of
(B,B)
-Manin matrix.

The matrix
M
gives the operator
({\mathbb{K}}^{n})^{*}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes({\mathbb{K}}^{m})^{*}
acting as
v^{i}\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{m}M^{i}_{j}\otimes w^{j}
, where
(v^{i})
and
(w^{j})
are bases dual to
(v_{i})
and
(w_{j})
. Any linear map
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})_{1}\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})_{1}
has such form for some matrix
M
. In fact, this map gives a homomorphism of graded algebras
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
iff
M
is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix (see
[
S
]
), we denote this homomorphism of graded algebras by
f_{M}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})
.

In the same way any linear map
\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{K}})_{1}\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})_{1}
has the form
{\mathbb{K}}^{m}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{n}
,
w_{j}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}M^{i}_{j}v_{i}
. It gives a graded homomorphism
\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
iff
M=(M^{i}_{j})
is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix, we denote this homomorphism by
f^{M}\colon\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\to\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})
.

Thus we have bijections between the sets
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{K}}),\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)
and the set of
(A,B)
-Manin matrices.

4.2.4.

Usual Manin matrices.
The Manin matrices introduced in the papers
[
CF
,
CFR
]
are
n\times m
Manin matrices for the pair of idempotents
(A_{n},A_{m})
. They correspond to the graded homomorphisms of polynomial algebras
{\mathbb{K}}[x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}]\to{\mathfrak{R}}[y^{1},\ldots,y^{m}]
or, equivalently, to the graded homomorphisms
S(V^{*})\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes S(W^{*})
, where
V
and
W
are
n
- and
m
-dimensional vector spaces.

4.3
Semi-linear spaces and their quantum analogue

To interpret representations of algebraic monoids and groups on (finite-dimensional) linear spaces in terms of Subsection
3.2
one needs to include both algebraic sets and linear spaces to a bigger monoidal category. More generally, one needs to do the same for the affine schemes. In this way we will get categories of spaces which are partially linear in some sense. We call them semi-linear spaces.

4.3.1.

Semi-linear algebraic sets.

Describe the first case of semi-linear spaces. For an algebraic set
X\in{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
and a linear space
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
consider their set-theoretic product
X\times V
as an algebraic set. Define morphisms between two such products
X\times V
and
Y\times W
as morphisms
F\colon X\times V\to Y\times W
in
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
that have the form

\displaystyle F(x,v)

\displaystyle=(\varphi(x),f(x,v)),\qquad\quad x\in X,\quad v\in V,\qquad\qquad\varphi\colon X\to Y,\qquad f\colon X\times V\to W,

\displaystyle f(x,\alpha v+v^{\prime})=\alpha f(x,v)+f(x,v^{\prime}),\quad\quad x\in X,\quad v,v^{\prime}\in V.

(4.55)

In other words, the morphism
F
is given by arbitrary morphisms
\varphi\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{AlgSet}}}(X,Y)
and
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{AlgSet}}}(X\times V,W)
such that
f(x,-)\colon V\to W
is linear for any
x\in X
. In this way we obtain a category
{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
with morphisms that partially satisfy the linearity condition, we call them
semi-linear maps
. We call the objects
X\times V\in{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}

semi-linear algebraic sets
.

4.3.2.

Monoidal product of semi-linear algebraic sets.
Let
X,X^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
and
V,V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
. Define monoidal product of
X\times V,X^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
as the object
(X\times X^{\prime})\times(V\otimes V^{\prime})
. For morphisms
F\colon X\times V\to Y\times W
and
F^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}\times W^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
, given by maps
\varphi\colon X\to Y
,
f\colon X\times V\to W
and
\varphi^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime}
,
f^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}\to W^{\prime}
, their monoidal product is
F^{\prime\prime}=F\otimes F^{\prime}\colon(X\times X^{\prime})\times(V\otimes V^{\prime})\to(Y\times Y^{\prime})\times(W\otimes W^{\prime})
given by
\varphi^{\prime\prime}=\varphi\times\varphi^{\prime}\colon X\times X\to Y\times Y
and
f^{\prime\prime}\colon(X\times X^{\prime})\times(V\otimes V^{\prime})\to W\otimes W^{\prime}
,
f^{\prime\prime}(x,x^{\prime},v\otimes v^{\prime})=f(x,v)\otimes f^{\prime}(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})
. In this way we obtain a symmetric monoidal category
({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}},\otimes)
with the unit object
0\times{\mathbb{K}}
.

The categories
({\mathbf{AlgSet}},\times)
and
({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)
can be regarded as monoidal subcategories of
({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}},\otimes)
embedded via the faithful symmetric strict monoidal functors
X\mapsto X\times{\mathbb{K}}
and
V\mapsto\{0\}\times V
. The latter functor is full, the former is not, i.e.
{\mathbf{FVect}}
is a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
, while the subcategory
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}\subset{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
is not full.

4.3.3.

Trivial vector bundles.

The category
{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
is equivalent to the category of trivial vector bundles over algebraic sets. The semi-linear algebraic set
X\times V
corresponds to the trivial bundle over
X
with the fibre
V
. A semi-linear map
F\colon X\times V\to Y\times W
given by the formula (
4.3
) is the vector bundle morphism
(\varphi,F)
in the sense that the diagram

\textstyle{X\times V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{Y\times W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{Y}

is commutative (the vertical arrows mean the projections) and the morphism
F
induces a linear map on each fibre (see
[
Lang
, III, § 1]
).
Category embeddings
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
and
{\mathbf{FVect}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
identify an algebraic set
X
with the
1
-dimensional trivial bundle over it, and a vector space
V
– with the trivial bundle over
\{0\}=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathbb{K}}
with the fibre
V
.

The main difference between the notions of semi-linear algebraic set and trivial vector bundle is that we consider the former ones not over the structure of algebraic set, but together with this structure. In particular, the monoidal product of two semi-linear algebraic sets
X\times V
and
X\times W
is not the same as the tensor product of the corresponding bundles over
X
. One can consider more general vector bundles in the same manner, but the category
{\mathbf{SLAffSch}}
is enough for out purposes, this is a minimal monoidal category that includes
({\mathbf{AlgSet}},\times)
and
({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
.

4.3.4.

Algebras of functions on semi-linear algebraic sets.
Let
X\in{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
and
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
. The algebra of regular functions on
X\times V
as on algebraic set is
A(X)\otimes SV^{*}
. It inherits the grading from
SV^{*}
as a tensor product of an algebra with a graded algebra (see p.
2.1
).
Let us denote
S^{*}(X\times V)=A(X)\otimes SV^{*}\in{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
. For a morphism of the form (
4.3
) we obtain the morphism of graded algebras with the reverse direction:
S^{*}(F)\colon A(Y)\otimes SW^{*}\to A(X)\otimes SV^{*}
, it is defined as

\displaystyle S^{*}(F)\colon s\mapsto\varphi^{*}(s)=s\cdot\varphi\in A(X),

\displaystyle\varphi^{*}(s)(x)=s\big(\varphi(x)\big),

(4.60)

\displaystyle S^{*}(F)\colon\xi\mapsto f^{*}(\xi)=\xi\cdot f\in A(X)\otimes V^{*},

\displaystyle f^{*}(\xi)(x,v)=\xi\big(f(x,v)\big),

(4.61)

where
s\in A(Y)
,
\xi\in W^{*}
,
x\in X
,
v\in V
. The formula (
4.60
) gives the usual embedding
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{CommAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Let
(v_{j})
,
(v^{j})
and
(w_{i})
,
(w^{i})
be dual bases of
V
,
V^{*}
and
W
,
W^{*}
respectively. Then
f(x,v_{j})=\sum_{i}f^{i}_{j}(x)w_{i}
for some
f^{i}_{j}\in A(X)
, so the map (
4.61
) has the form
f^{*}(w^{i})=\sum_{j}f^{i}_{j}\otimes v^{j}\in A(X)\otimes V^{*}
.

We obtain a lax monoidal functor
S^{*}\colon({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
. In particular, any algebraic set
X
as the object
X\times{\mathbb{K}}
corresponds to the graded algebra
S^{*}(X)=A(X)\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[u]
and we have the natural isomorphism
S^{*}(X\times V)\cong S^{*}(X)\circ S^{*}(V)
.

4.3.5.

Semi-linear affine schemes.

Let us generalise the semi-linear spaces to the case of affine schemes.
Among the commutative semi-connected quadratic algebras consider the algebras of the form
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes SV^{*}
where
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{CommAlg}}
,
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
. Such algebra can be interpreted as the ‘function algebra’ on the affine scheme
X\times V
, where
X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}
. This is an affine scheme which corresponds to the algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes SV^{*}
and has an additional structure given by the grading of this algebra. We call such schemes
semi-linear affine schemes
. By definition they are objects of the category
{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
corresponding to the graded algebras
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes SV^{*}
, where
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{CommAlg}}
,
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
. The embedding
{\mathbf{SLAffSch}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
extends the functor
S^{*}\colon{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
,
S^{*}(X\times V)={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes SV^{*}
, since
{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
is a subcategory of
{\mathbf{SLAffSch}}
, so we also denote it by
S^{*}
.

Let
{\mathfrak{S}}\in{\mathbf{CommAlg}}
,
Y=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{S}}
and
W\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
.
A morphism
X\times V\to Y\times W
in
{\mathbf{SLAffSch}}
is given by a graded homomorphism
{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes SW^{*}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes SV^{*}
generated by an algebra homomorphism
\alpha\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
and a linear map
t\colon W^{*}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}
. In the order
1
it has the form

\displaystyle(\alpha,t)\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes W^{*}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*},

\displaystyle(\alpha,t)(s\otimes\xi)=\alpha(s)t(\xi).

(4.62)

For a morphism in
{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
of the form (
4.3
) we have
\alpha=\varphi^{*}
and
t=f^{*}
,
(\alpha,t)=F^{*}
.

Remark 4.4
.

The semi-linear affine scheme
X\times V
can be identified with a quasi-coherent sheaf corresponding to the free
{\mathfrak{R}}
-module
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
(or rather to its dual
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}
). The category
{\mathbf{SLAffSch}}
is equivalent to the category of such free sheaves (
{\mathfrak{R}}
-modules) with appropriate morphisms. This generalises the equivalence described in p.
4.3
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}},{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{CommAlg}}
,
X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}
,
X^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
,
V,V^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
. Define the monoidal product of the semi-linear affine schemes
X\times V
and
X^{\prime}\times V^{\prime}
as the semi-linear affine scheme
(X\times X^{\prime})\times(V\otimes V^{\prime})
with the function algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes S^{*}(V\otimes V^{\prime})
. Note that the embedding
{\mathbf{SLAffSch}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a fully faithful symmetric lax monoidal functor
S^{*}\colon({\mathbf{SLAffSch}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
.

4.3.6.

Semi-linear algebraic monoids and semi-linear affine monoid schemes.

A monoid in
({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}},\otimes)
is a semi-linear algebraic set
X\times V\in{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
with the semi-linear maps
\mu_{X\times V}\colon(X\times X)\times(V\otimes V)\to X\times V
,
\eta_{X\times V}\colon{\mathbb{K}}\to X\times V
which have the form

\displaystyle\mu_{X\times V}(x,y,u\otimes v)=\big(xy,f(x,y,u\otimes v)\big),

\displaystyle\eta_{X\times V}(1)=\big(e,1_{V}\big),

(4.63)

where
xy=\mu_{X}(x,y)
,
e=\eta_{X}(0)
are defined by a monoid
(X,\mu_{X},\eta_{X})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{AlgSet}},\times)
and
f\colon X\times X\times(V\otimes V)\to V
is a morphism in
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}
linear in the last argument such that
f(e,x,1_{V}\otimes v)=v=f(x,e,v\otimes 1_{V})
,
f\big(x,yz,u\otimes f(y,z,v\otimes w)\big)=f\big(xy,z,f(x,y,u\otimes v)\otimes w\big)
,
x,y,z\in X
,
u,v,w\in V
. Denote by
V_{x}
the copy of
V
considered as a fibre over
x\in X
, then for any
x,y\in X
we obtain the linear map
f_{x,y}\colon V_{x}\otimes V_{y}\to V_{xy}
,
f_{x,y}(u\otimes v)=f(x,y,u\otimes v)
, and the last condition is equivalent to the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{V_{x}\otimes V_{y}\otimes V_{z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f_{y,z}}
\scriptstyle{f_{x,y}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{V_{x}\otimes V_{yz}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{x,yz}}
\textstyle{V_{xy}\otimes V_{z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447ptf_{xy,z}}
\textstyle{V_{xyz}}

while
1_{V}
belongs to
V_{e}
. If
X=\{e\}
is a one-point set, then to equip
V=X\times V
with a monoid structure is the same as to choose the algebra
(V,f_{e,e},1)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
. If
V={\mathbb{K}}
, then a monoid structure on
X\times V=X\times{\mathbb{K}}
is given by an algebraic monoid
(X,\mu_{X},e)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{AlgSet}},\times)
with collection of coefficients
p_{x,y}\in{\mathbb{K}}
, while
q\in{\mathbb{K}}\backslash\{0\}
satisfying
p_{x,yz}p_{y,z}=p_{xy,z}p_{x,y}
and
p_{e,x}=p_{x,e}=q^{-1}
(the linear map
f_{x,y}\colon{\mathbb{K}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}\to{\mathbb{K}}
is a multiplication by the coefficient
p_{x,y}
, while
1_{V}=q
). The case of a usual algebraic monoid corresponds to the values
p_{x,y}=q=1
. In this way we see that the categorical embeddings
{\mathbf{FVect}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
and
{\mathbf{AlgSet}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{SLAlgSet}}
induce the fully faithful functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}},\otimes)
and the faithful functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{AlgSet}},\times)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLAlgSet}},\otimes)
, which are symmetric strong monoidal due to p.
2.4
.

Let
{\mathfrak{R}}=A(X)
. The homomorphisms
\alpha=\mu_{X}^{*}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}
and
\beta=\eta_{X}^{*}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathbb{K}}
gives the structure of coalgebra on
{\mathfrak{R}}
. The linear maps
t=f^{*}\colon V^{*}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}
and
\varepsilon_{V^{*}}=1_{V}\colon V^{*}\to{\mathbb{K}}
make the diagrams

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\alpha,t)}
\scriptstyle{(\alpha,t)}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(23)}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes(\alpha,t)}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\alpha,t)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(34)}\sigma^{(23)}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{(34)}\sigma^{(45)}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}}

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\alpha,t)}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{K}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}\otimes V^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\varepsilon_{V^{*}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\beta\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\varepsilon_{V^{*}}}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}}

to be commutative, where
\sigma^{(i,i+1)}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes\sigma\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
. Since
({\mathfrak{R}},\alpha,\beta)
is a comonoid in
({\mathbf{CommAlg}},\otimes)
, it is enough to check the commutativity of the diagrams (
4.3
) by substituting the elements of the form
1\otimes\xi\in{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}
.

Now consider monoids in
({\mathbf{SLAffSch}},\otimes)
, which we call
semi-linear affine monoid schemes
. Let
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{CommAlg}}
,
V\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
and
X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{R}}
. A structure of monoid on
X\times V
is defined by the morphisms
\mu_{X\times V}\colon(X\times V)\otimes(X\times V)\to X\times V
and
\eta_{X\times V}\colon{\mathbb{K}}\to X\times V
, which are given by graded homomorphisms
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes S(V^{*})\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes S(V^{*})\otimes S(V^{*})
and
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes S(V^{*})\to{\mathbb{K}}[u]
. The zero and first order components of these homomorphisms are
\alpha\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}
,
(\alpha,t)\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}
and
\beta\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathbb{K}}
,
(\beta,\varepsilon_{V^{*}})\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\to{\mathbb{K}}
, where
t\colon V^{*}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V^{*}\otimes V^{*}
and
\varepsilon_{V^{*}}\colon V^{*}\to{\mathbb{K}}
are linear maps. They give a structure of a monoid on
X\times V
iff
({\mathfrak{R}},\alpha,\beta)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{CommAlg}},\otimes)
and the diagrams (
4.3
) commute.
The embedding
{\mathbf{AffSch}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{SLAffSch}}
induces the faithful symmetric strong monoidal functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{AffSch}},\times)\to\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{SLAffSch}},\otimes)
.

4.3.7.

Quantum semi-linear spaces.

The ‘algebra of functions’ on a semi-linear space
X\times V
is the tensor product of a quadratic algebra
S^{*}V
with a commutative algebra that is the ‘algebra of functions’ on
X
. By following the Manin’s idea described in the beginning of Section
3
we call
quantum semi-linear space
any semi-connected quadratic algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
(where
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
,
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
) considered as an object of the dual category
{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The finite-dimensional case corresponds to the objects of
{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
. The quantum linear spaces correspond to the connected case
{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathbb{K}}
. In Section
5
we will construct Quantum Representation Theory as the theory of representations of monoids in
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
on the objects of a monoidal subcategory
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

The composition of the embeddings
{\mathbf{AffSch}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{SLAffSch}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
is a faithful symmetric strong monoidal functor
({\mathbf{AffSch}},\times)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
, its dual is the functor
({\mathbf{CommAlg}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
, which maps
{\mathfrak{R}}
to
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[u]
. Thus, we consider an affine non-commutative space
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}^{\mathrm{op}}
as the quantum semi-linear space
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[u]\in{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
.

Any morphism
f\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
in
{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
between arbitrary semi-connected quadratic algebras
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{S}}\otimes\big(TW/(S)\big)
and
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\big(TV/(R)\big)
is uniquely defined by its zero and first order components
\alpha=f_{0}\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
and
f_{1}\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes W\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
.
We have
f_{1}=(\alpha,t)
in the sense that
f_{1}(s\otimes w)=\alpha(s)t(w)=t(w)\alpha(s)
, where
t\colon W\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
is defined by the formula
t(w)=f_{1}(1\otimes w)
. Since
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
is the quotient of
({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V)\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V)={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V\otimes V
over
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes R
, the linear map
f_{2}\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
is induced by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes W\otimes W=({\mathfrak{S}}\otimes W)\otimes_{\mathfrak{S}}({\mathfrak{S}}\otimes W)\xrightarrow{f_{1}\otimes_{\mathfrak{S}}f_{1}}({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V)\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V)={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V\otimes V,

\displaystyle s\otimes w\otimes w^{\prime}=(s\otimes w)\otimes_{{\mathfrak{S}}}(1\otimes w^{\prime})\mapsto\big(\alpha(s)t(w)\big)\otimes_{{\mathfrak{R}}}t(w^{\prime})=\alpha(s)\big(t(w)\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}t(w^{\prime})\big),

\displaystyle 1\otimes w\otimes w^{\prime}\mapsto\big(t(w)\otimes_{\mathfrak{R}}t(w^{\prime})\big)=(t\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}t)(w\otimes w^{\prime}),

where
t\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}t\colon W\otimes W\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V\otimes V
is the composition

\displaystyle W\otimes W\xrightarrow{t\otimes t}{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\sigma_{V,{\mathfrak{R}}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}}{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V\otimes V\xrightarrow{\mu_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}}{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V\otimes V.

An algebra homomorphism
\alpha\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
and a linear map
t\colon W\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V
define a homomorphism
f\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}
iff

\displaystyle\alpha(s)t(w)=t(w)\alpha(s)\qquad\forall\,s\in{\mathfrak{S}},\,w\in W

(4.80)

and
(t\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}t)S\subset{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes R
. In particular,
({\mathcal{B}},\Delta,\varepsilon)
is a comonoid in
({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
iff the zero and first order components of the graded homomorphisms
\Delta\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{B}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
and
\varepsilon\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathbb{K}}[u]
satisfy the following conditions:
\alpha=\Delta_{0}\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\to{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}
and
\beta=\varepsilon_{0}\colon{\mathfrak{S}}\to{\mathbb{K}}
are algebra homomorphisms,
\Delta_{1}(s\otimes w)=\alpha(s)t(w)
and
\varepsilon_{1}(s\otimes w)=\varepsilon_{0}(s)\varepsilon_{W}(w)
for some linear maps
t\colon W\to{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes W\otimes W
,
\varepsilon_{W}\colon W\to{\mathbb{K}}
subjected to the commutativity condition (
4.80
), the inclusion conditions
(t\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}t)S\subset{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes{\mathfrak{S}}\otimes\sigma^{(23)}(S\otimes W^{\otimes 2}+W^{\otimes 2}\otimes S)
,
(\varepsilon_{W}\otimes\varepsilon_{W})S=0
and making the diagrams (
4.3
) to be commutative (where
V^{*}=W
and
{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathfrak{S}}
).

A coaction of the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=({\mathcal{A}},\Delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
on an object
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
is a graded homomorphism
\delta\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
. Its zero component
\delta_{0}\colon{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
should be coaction of
({\mathcal{A}}_{0},\Delta_{0},\varepsilon_{0})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)
on
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
and the first order component is
\delta_{1}=(\delta_{0},p)
for some linear map
p\colon W\to{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\otimes V\otimes W
. To obtain the condition for
p
one needs to take first order components of the morphisms in the commutative diagrams (
2.4
), this leads to diagrams similar to (
4.3
).

4.4
Internal hom for quantum linear spaces

The next ingredient, which we need to construct quantum representations, is a quantum analogue of the internal hom of vector spaces. This is an internal hom in
({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
, i.e. an internal cohom in
({\mathbf{FQA}},\circ)
. We need also its generalised version in the sense of p.
3.1
.

4.4.1.

Internal cohom and universal Manin matrix.

It was shown in
[
Man87
,
Man88
]
that the category
{\mathbf{FQA}}
is coclosed with respect to the Manin product, namely, proved the existence of a natural isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}}\bullet{\mathcal{B}}^{!},{\mathcal{C}})\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\circ{\mathcal{C}})
. Hence the internal cohom for
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
can be defined as
3
3

3

Manin denoted the cohom-functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{FQA}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}
as
\underline{\hom}
.

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})={\mathcal{B}}^{!}\bullet{\mathcal{A}}.

(4.81)

Let us calculate it for
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
and
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
, where
A\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(V^{\otimes 2})
and
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W^{\otimes 2})
are idempotents and
V={\mathbb{K}}^{n}
,
W={\mathbb{K}}^{m}
. Since
{\mathcal{B}}^{!}=\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{K}})=TW/\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(1-B)\big)
and
{\mathcal{A}}=TV^{*}/(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits A^{*})
we have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{!}\bullet{\mathcal{A}}=T(W\otimes V^{*})/\big(\sigma^{(23)}(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(1-B)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits A^{*})\big),

(4.82)

where
\sigma^{(23)}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\sigma\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
(the indices
2,3
mean that
\sigma
acts on the second and third tensor factors). Let
(v_{i})
and
(w_{j})
be bases of
V
and
W
respectively. Let
(v^{i})
be basis of
V^{*}
dual to
(v_{i})
. Then the basis of
W\otimes V^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
consists of
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}=w_{j}\otimes v^{i}
. The subspace
\sigma^{(23)}(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(1-B)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits A^{*})\subset W\otimes V^{*}\otimes W\otimes V^{*}
is spanned by the vectors

\sigma^{(23)}\sum_{k,l,s,t}(1-B)^{kl}_{ij}A^{pq}_{st}w_{k}\otimes w_{l}\otimes v^{s}\otimes v^{t}=\sum_{k,l,s,t}A^{pq}_{st}({\mathcal{M}}_{k}^{s}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}_{l}^{t})(1-B)^{kl}_{ij},

where
(1-B)^{kl}_{ij}=\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{l}_{j}-B^{kl}_{ij}
. Therefore, the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})
is generated by
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
with the quadratic relation

\displaystyle A{\mathcal{M}}^{(1)}{\mathcal{M}}^{(2)}(1-B)=0,

(4.83)

where
{\mathcal{M}}
is the matrix with entries
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
. The relation (
4.83
) means exactly that
{\mathcal{M}}
is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix over
{\mathfrak{R}}=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})\big)
. We call it
universal
(A,B)
-Manin matrix
. Note also that

\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})\big)={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})^{!}\bullet{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})=\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}})^{!}\bullet\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{K}})=\\
=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big(\Xi_{A}({\mathbb{K}}),\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\big).

(4.84)

4.4.2.

Connection with the Manin matrices.
In
[
S
]
we denote by
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
the algebra (
4.84
) considered as an object of
{\mathbf{Alg}}
. This algebra was interpreted there via left adjoints to the functors
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\colon{\mathbf{Alg}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
and
\Xi_{A}\colon{\mathbf{Alg}}\to{\mathbf{FQA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
. In particular, we derived the natural bijections

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{GrAlg}}\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathfrak{R}})\big)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Alg}}({\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B},{\mathfrak{R}})\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{GrAlg}}\big(\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{K}}),\Xi_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\big),

(4.85)

which can be described as follows.
The left and right sets consists of the graded homomorphisms
f_{M}
and
f^{M}
for
(A,B)
-Manin matrices
M
over
{\mathfrak{R}}
(see p.
4.2
). A formula
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}\mapsto M^{i}_{j}
gives a homomorphism
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
(in
{\mathbf{Alg}}
) iff
M=(M^{i}_{j})
is an
(A,B)
-Manin matrix.

Consider the case when
{\mathfrak{R}}
is also a quadratic algebra:
{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})
for some idempotent
C
. Note that
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\big)_{k}={\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\otimes{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})_{k}
, so the algebra
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\big)
does not coincide with
{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\otimes{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
as graded algebra in the sense of the Manin’s operation
\otimes
(see p.
4.1
). Consider the subset of the set (
4.85
) corresponding to the
(A,B)
-Manin matrices
M
with entries of the first order:
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})_{1}
. As a subset of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{GrAlg}}\Big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\big)\Big)
it consists of the graded homomorphisms
{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\big)
which factors through the morphism
{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\circ{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\big)
. This subset can be identified with the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{GrAlg}}\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\circ{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\big)
. As a subset of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{Alg}}\Big({\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B},{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\big)\Big)
it consists of homomorphisms
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})
which preserve the grading, where the grading of the algebra
{\mathfrak{U}}_{A,B}
arises, if one considers it as the cohom-object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})\big)
. This leads to the adjunction from the beginning of p.
4.4
, in the notations of this paragraph it takes the form

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\circ{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\big)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\Big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})\big),{\mathfrak{X}}_{C}({\mathbb{K}})\Big),

(4.86)

this isomorphism is induced by the isomorphism (
4.85
).

4.4.3.

Internal hom for finite-dimensional vector spaces.
Let us regard how the internal hom of quantum linear spaces agrees with the internal hom of vector spaces. Remind that the internal hom-object
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
in
{\mathbf{Vect}}
is the set
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
equipped with the usual structure of vector space (see p.
3.1
). In the case
W\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
we have natural linear isomorphism
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)=W^{*}\otimes V
. By applying the strong monoidal functor
(-)^{*}_{1}\colon({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)
to the internal hom (
4.81
) we obtain exactly
\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\big)^{*}_{1}=W^{*}\otimes V=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
, where
W={\mathcal{B}}_{1}^{*}
,
V={\mathcal{A}}_{1}^{*}
. In the notations of Subsection
3.3
it means that the morphisms
\Phi_{{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}}}
for the strong monoidal functor
(-)^{*}_{1}\colon({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)\to({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)
are isomorphisms.

On the other hand the quadratic algebra
S^{*}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
does not coincide with the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(S^{*}W,S^{*}V)=(S^{*}W)^{!}\bullet S^{*}V=\Lambda(W)\bullet S^{*}(V)
, which means that
\Phi_{W,V}
for the lax monoidal functor
S^{*}\colon({\mathbf{FVect}},\otimes)\to({\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
are not isomorphisms.

4.4.4.

Coevaluation and cocomposition morphisms.

Let
(v_{i})
,
(w_{j})
be the bases of
V,W\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
and let
(v^{i})
,
(w^{j})
be the dual bases of
V^{*}
,
W^{*}
. The elements
v^{i}
,
w^{j}
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}=w_{j}\otimes v^{i}
are generators of the algebras
{\mathcal{A}}=TV^{*}/(R)
,
{\mathcal{B}}=TW^{*}/(S)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})
. In these terms the coevaluation (
3.14
) (for
X={\mathcal{A}}
,
Y={\mathcal{B}}
) has the form (see
[
Man88
]
)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}}\big)\circ{\mathcal{B}},

\displaystyle v^{i}\mapsto\sum_{j}{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}\otimes w^{j}.

(4.87)

Let
{\mathcal{C}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
and
(z_{l})
be a basis of
Z={\mathcal{C}}_{1}^{*}
. Then
{\mathcal{N}}^{j}_{l}=z_{l}\otimes w^{j}
and
{\mathcal{K}}^{i}_{l}=z_{l}\otimes v^{i}
are the generators of the algebras
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{B}})
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{A}})
. The cocomposition morphism (
3.16
) (for
X={\mathcal{A}}
,
Y={\mathcal{B}}
,
Z={\mathcal{C}}
) reads (see
[
S
]
)

\displaystyle d=d_{{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{C}}}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{A}})\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\circ\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{B}}),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{i}_{l}\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}^{j}_{l}.

(4.88)

Let
B=(B^{ij}_{kl})_{i,j,k,l=1}^{m}
be a matrix idempotent such that the elements
\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{m}B^{ij}_{kl}w_{i}\otimes w_{j}
span the subspace
S\subset W\otimes W
, then
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}})
is the quadratic algebra generated by
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}=w_{j}\otimes w^{j}
with the relations
B{\mathcal{M}}^{(1)}{\mathcal{M}}^{(2)}(1-B)=0
, where
{\mathcal{M}}=({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j})_{i,j=1}^{m}
is the universal
B
-Manin matrix. The comonoid
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{FQA}},\circ)
is the quadratic algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}})
with the structure morphisms
d_{\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\circ\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})
and
v_{\mathcal{B}}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathbb{K}}[u]
defined on generators as

\displaystyle d_{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j})=\sum_{k=1}^{m}{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{M}}^{k}_{j},

\displaystyle v_{\mathcal{B}}({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j})=\delta^{i}_{j}u.

(4.89)

Since
{\mathcal{B}}^{!}=\Xi_{B}({\mathbb{K}})=TW/\big(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits(1-B)\big)={\mathfrak{X}}_{1-B^{\top}}({\mathbb{K}})
is generated by
w_{1},\ldots,w_{m}\in W
, the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}}^{!})
is generated by the elements
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{ij}=w^{j}\otimes w_{i}\in W^{*}\otimes W
. The latter ones are entries of the universal
(1-B^{\top})
-Manin matrix
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}=(\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{ij})
transposed to the universal
B
-Manin matrix
{\mathcal{M}}
. Due to the formulae (
4.89
) this implies

\displaystyle d_{{\mathcal{B}}^{!}}=d_{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathrm{cop}},

\displaystyle v_{{\mathcal{B}}^{!}}=v_{\mathcal{B}}.

(4.90)

4.4.5.

Infinite-dimensional case.

Let
{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
. Then
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=T\widetilde{V}/(\widetilde{R})
and
{\mathcal{B}}=TW/(S)
for some
V,\widetilde{V}\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
,
W\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
,
R\subset V^{\otimes 2}
,
\widetilde{R}\subset\widetilde{V}^{\otimes 2}
,
S\subset W^{\otimes 2}
. Let us prove that there exists
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})
in the sense of p.
3.1
, where the role of parameter is played by
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

For any
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
the functor
-\circ{\mathcal{B}}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}
has a left adjoint
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},-)\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}
. We have
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})=TV^{\prime}/(R^{\prime})
where
V^{\prime}=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)
and

\displaystyle R^{\prime}=\{U\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)^{\otimes 2}\mid U(W^{\otimes 2})\subset R,U(S)=0\}.

(4.91)

Proof.
Let
(w_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
and
(w^{i})_{i=1}^{m}
be dual bases of
W^{*}
and
(W^{*})^{*}=W
respectively. These bases give identifications
W=({\mathbb{K}}^{m})^{*}
and
W^{*}={\mathbb{K}}^{m}
. Let
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{m})
be a matrix idempotent such that
S=\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits B^{*}
, then
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})=TW/(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits B^{*})
. By identifying
W^{*}\otimes W
with
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)
we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}w_{i}\otimes w^{i}\otimes w_{j}\otimes w^{j}=\sum_{i,j,k,l}\big(B_{ij}^{kl}+(1-B)_{ij}^{kl}\big)w_{k}\otimes w^{i}\otimes w_{l}\otimes w^{j},

(4.92)

where the coefficients
B^{ij}_{kl}\in{\mathbb{K}}
and
(1-B)^{ij}_{kl}=\delta^{i}_{k}\delta^{j}_{l}-B^{ij}_{kl}
are entries of the operators
B
and
1-B
respectively.
Consider the linear map
\eta_{V}\colon V\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\otimes W
defined in the proof of Prop.
3.2
, that is
\eta_{V}(v)=\sum_{i}(w_{i}\otimes v)\otimes w^{i}
. It generates a morphism
\eta\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\circ{\mathcal{B}}
with the first order component
\eta_{1}=\eta_{V}
. Indeed, for any element
r=\sum_{s}x_{s}\otimes y_{s}\in R
we have

\sigma^{(23)}(\eta_{V}\otimes\eta_{V})r=\sum_{s,i,j}(w_{i}\otimes x_{s})\otimes(w_{j}\otimes y_{s})\otimes w^{i}\otimes w^{j}=\\
=\sum_{s,i,j,k,l}\big(B_{ij}^{kl}+(1-B)_{ij}^{kl}\big)w_{k}\otimes x_{s}\otimes w_{l}\otimes y_{s}\otimes w^{i}\otimes w^{j}.

(4.93)

Since
B_{ij}^{kl}w^{i}\otimes w^{j}\in S
and
(1-B)_{ij}^{kl}w_{k}\otimes x_{s}\otimes w_{l}\otimes y_{s}\in R^{\prime}
, the element (
4.93
) belongs to
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)^{\otimes 2}\otimes S+R^{\prime}\otimes W^{\otimes 2}
.

Now we need to prove that for any
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
and
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}})
there exists a unique
h\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}}),\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\big)
making the diagram

\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta\hskip 16.38895pt}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\circ{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h\circ\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{B}}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}}

commutative. For purposes of the next paragraph we do it for a slightly more general case
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes T\widetilde{V}/(\widetilde{R})
for some vector space
\widetilde{V}\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
and subspace
\widetilde{R}\subset\widetilde{V}\otimes\widetilde{V}
. We have
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{1}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{V}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=T_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}}{\mathcal{A}}_{1}/(\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{R})
.
Note that any graded homomorphism
g\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
is uniquely determined by its first order component
g_{1}\colon V\to\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{V}
and, conversely, this component gives the whole graded homomorphism
g\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
if it is linear and satisfies
(g_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}g_{1})R\subset\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{R}
, where
g_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}g_{1}\colon V\otimes V\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{V}\otimes\widetilde{V}
is defined as in p.
4.3
, that is
g_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}g_{1}=(\mu_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{V}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\widetilde{V}})\cdot\sigma^{(23)}\cdot(g_{1}\otimes g_{1})
. By using the natural isomorphism
({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}})\circ{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes({\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}})
we obtain
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes T(\widetilde{V}\otimes W)/(R_{\mathrm{w}})
, where
R_{\mathrm{w}}=\sigma^{(23)}(\widetilde{V}^{\otimes 2}\otimes S+\widetilde{R}\otimes W^{\otimes 2})
. In particular,
(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}})_{1}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{V}\otimes W=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W^{*},\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{V})
.

Due to Prop.
3.2
there exists a unique linear map
h_{1}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\to\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{V}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{1}\hskip 16.38895pt}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\otimes W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{1}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{V}\otimes W}

commute, it has the form
h_{1}(w_{i}\otimes v)=f_{1}(v)(w_{i})
. This implies the uniqueness of
h
. Let us prove its existence. Let
U\in R^{\prime}
, it has the form
U=\sum\limits_{s,i,j}U_{s}^{ij}w_{i}\otimes x_{s}\otimes w_{j}\otimes y_{s}
for some
U_{s}^{ij}\in{\mathbb{K}}
and
x_{s},y_{s}\in V
such that
x_{s}\otimes y_{s}\in R
. Since
U(S)=0
we have
\sum\limits_{s,i,j}U_{s}^{ij}B_{ij}^{kl}x_{s}\otimes y_{s}=0
, which implies
U=\sum\limits_{s,i,j,k,l}U_{s}^{ij}(1-B)_{ij}^{kl}w_{k}\otimes x_{s}\otimes w_{l}\otimes y_{s}
. Hence

\displaystyle(h_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}h_{1})U=\sum_{s,i,j,k,l}U_{s}^{ij}(1-B)_{ij}^{kl}(f_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}f_{1})(x_{s}\otimes y_{s})(w_{k}\otimes w_{l}),

(4.102)

where
(f_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}f_{1})(x_{s}\otimes y_{s})
belongs to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{V}\otimes W\otimes\widetilde{V}\otimes W=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W^{*}\otimes W^{*},\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{V}\otimes\widetilde{V})
.
Since
(f_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\otimes}}f_{1})R\subset R_{\mathrm{w}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle(f_{1}\otimes f_{1})(x_{s}\otimes y_{s})\in(f_{1}\otimes f_{1})R\subset\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\otimes\sigma_{V,W}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{W})(V\otimes V\otimes S+\widetilde{R}\otimes W\otimes W).

The formula
(1-B^{*})(\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits B^{*})=0
implies
\sum_{k,l}(1-B)_{ij}^{kl}(w_{k}\otimes w_{l})(S)=0
, so we derive
(h_{1}\otimes h_{1})U\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\widetilde{R}
.

We proved that
\eta
is a universal arrow from
{\mathcal{B}}
to the functor
-\circ{\mathcal{B}}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}\to{\mathbf{QA}}
, hence this functor has a left adjoint
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},-)
, which acts on an object
{\mathcal{A}}
as defined in the proposition (see
[
MacLane
, § 4.1, Th. 2 (ii)]
). ∎

Remark 4.6
.

The proof of Prop.
4.5
can be directly generalised to the case of arbitrary connected affinely generated graded algebras
{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{B}}
such that
{\mathcal{B}}
is finely generated:
\dim{\mathcal{B}}_{1}<\infty
.

4.4.6.

Semi-connected case.
The generalised internal cohom found in the previous paragraph can be extended to the case
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
,
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}
. Remind that any
{\mathcal{D}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
has the form
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
for
{\mathfrak{R}}={\mathcal{D}}_{0}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
.

Proposition 4.7
.

For any
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
the functor
-\circ{\mathcal{B}}\colon{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}\to{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
has a left adjoint
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},-)\colon{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}\to{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
. Let
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
for some
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
. Then
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{D}})={\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\in{\mathbf{QA}}
is defined as in Prop.
4.5
.

Proof.
Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
. For an arbitrary graded homomorphism
\widehat{f}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
consider its zero component
\alpha=\widehat{f}_{0}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{Alg}}}({\mathfrak{R}},\widetilde{A}_{0})
and its restriction
f\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{GrAlg}}}({\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
to the subalgebra
{\mathcal{A}}\subset{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
. They satisfy the commutativity condition
\alpha(r)f(a_{1})=f(a_{1})\alpha(r)

\;\forall\,r\in{\mathfrak{R}},\,a_{1}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
. Any pair of such morphisms subjected to the commutativity condition uniquely defines a morphism
\widehat{f}=(\alpha,f)\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{GrAlg}}}({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
(see
[
S
, § 2.5]
, cf. p.
4.3
).

Let
\widehat{f}=(\alpha,f)\colon{\mathcal{D}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
be a graded homomorphism to an algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
, where
\alpha\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
and
f\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
are morphisms subjected to the commutativity condition.
As it was proved in p.
4.4
there exists a unique graded homomorphism
h\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
making the digram (
4.4
) commutative. By substituting
f(v)=\sum_{i}h_{1}(w_{i}\otimes v)\otimes w^{i}
,
v\in V
, to the commutativity condition
\alpha(r)f(v)=f(v)\alpha(r)
we obtain the commutativity condition for the pair
(\alpha,h)
, so it gives a graded homomorphism
\widehat{h}=(\alpha,h)\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
and we obtain the commutative diagram

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\eta\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 16.38895pt}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{f}=(\alpha,f)}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\circ{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widehat{h}\circ\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{B}=(\alpha,h\circ\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{B}})}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}}

The uniqueness of
h
implies the uniqueness of
\widehat{h}
. ∎

4.4.7.

Internal cohom on the morphisms.

Prop.
4.7
implies the existence of the generalised internal cohom-functor
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\colon{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}\to{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
and gives its values on objects. Let us calculate this functor on morphisms. Let
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
,
{\mathfrak{R}},{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
and
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
. Set
W={\mathcal{B}}_{1}
,
W^{\prime}={\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{1}
,
V={\mathcal{A}}_{1}
,
V^{\prime}={\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{1}
. Consider arbitrary morphisms
f\colon{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{B}}
and
g\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
. They are uniquely determined by the components
f_{1}\colon W^{\prime}\to W
,
\alpha=g_{0}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
and
g_{1}=(\alpha,t)\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes V\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes V^{\prime}
, where
t\colon V\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes V^{\prime}
. Then the morphism
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})
is uniquely determined by the components

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)_{0}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime},

(4.107)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)_{1}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W^{\prime},V^{\prime}).

(4.108)

Proposition 4.8
.

The components (
4.107
), (
4.108
) equal

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)_{0}=\alpha,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)_{1}=(\alpha,t_{*}f_{1}^{*}),

(4.109)

where
g_{0}=\alpha
,
g_{1}=(\alpha,t)
and the operator
t_{*}f_{1}^{*}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W^{\prime},V^{\prime})
is the composition

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W,V)\xrightarrow{f_{1}^{*}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W^{\prime},V)\xrightarrow{t_{*}}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W^{\prime},{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes V^{\prime})={\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf hom}}\nolimits(W^{\prime},V^{\prime}).

(4.110)

Proof.
Due to the formulae (
3.15
) and (
3.44
) we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)\circ\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\big)\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits=\vartheta\big(\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)\big)=\big(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\circ f\big)\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits\cdot g,

(4.111)

where
\mathop{\mathrm{coev}}\nolimits
coincides with the horizontal arrow in (
4.4
). This gives us the commuting diagram

\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\eta}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\eta\hskip 16.38895pt\hskip 16.38895pt}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})\circ{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\circ f}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{A}})\circ{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)\circ\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime})\circ{\mathcal{B}}}

By taking zero and first order components in this diagram we obtain (
4.109
). ∎

Consider the case
{\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
. Up to isomorphisms we have
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})
,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})
,
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
,
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})
for some matrix idempotents
A,A^{\prime},B,B^{\prime}
. Then the morphisms
f\colon{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{B}}
and
g\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
have the form

\displaystyle f=f_{K}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})

and

\displaystyle g=(\alpha,f_{M})\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathfrak{R}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime})

(4.116)

respectively for a
(B^{\prime},B)
-Manin matrix
K
over
{\mathbb{K}}
, an
(A,A^{\prime})
-Manin matrix
M
over
{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
and a homomorphism
\alpha\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}
such that
\alpha(r)M^{i}_{j}=M^{i}_{j}\alpha(r)

\;\forall\,r\in{\mathfrak{R}}
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
be the universal
(A,B)
- and
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
-Manin matrices respectively. Their entries are generators of
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})\big)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})\big)
. The graded homomorphism

\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{A}({\mathbb{K}})\big)\to{\mathfrak{R}}^{\prime}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}}),{\mathfrak{X}}_{A^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})\big)

acts on the generators as

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)\colon r\otimes 1\mapsto\alpha(r)\otimes 1,\qquad r\in{\mathfrak{R}},

(4.117)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,g)\colon 1\otimes{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}\mapsto\sum_{a,b}M^{i}_{a}\otimes{\mathcal{N}}^{a}_{b}K^{b}_{j}.

(4.118)

Note that the matrix
M{\mathcal{N}}K
appeared in (
4.118
) is a
(A,B)
-Manin matrix, which follows directly from
[
S
, Prop. 2.26]
.

5
Quantum Representation Theory

By taking the category
{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}}
with the Manin product ‘
\circ
’ and parameter category
{\mathbf{P}}={\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}
we obtain a class of representations on quantum linear spaces.
Quantum Representation Theory
presented here investigates representations for this case and, more generally, for
{\mathbf{C}}=({\mathbf{GrAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
. It can be regarded as a generalisation of Representation Theory on the usual vector spaces (classical representation theory). The latter one can be embedded into Quantum Representation Theory in two ways: by the functor
S^{*}
or
T^{*}
. The binary and duality operations with representations can be generalised to the quantum case.

5.1
Quantum representations

First we describe objects which we are going to ‘represent’. In the classical representation theory these were algebraic monoids and algebras. In the quantum case we need to consider monoids in
({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
or, more generally, in
({\mathbf{GrAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
. We call these monoids
quantum algebras
.

5.1.1.

Quantum monoids.

A quantum analogue of algebraic monoids is a comonoid in the monoidal category
({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)
. This is an algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}\in{\mathbf{Alg}}
with comultiplication
\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathfrak{R}}
and counit
\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}}\colon{\mathfrak{R}}\to{\mathbb{K}}
.
These are algebra homomorphisms satisfying the equations
(\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{R}})\cdot\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{R}}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}})\cdot\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}
and the coassociativity condition
(\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{R}})\cdot\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}})\cdot\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}
. Such comonoids
({\mathfrak{R}},\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}},\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}})
are exactly the bialgebras:
\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)=\mathop{\mathbf{Bimon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
.

To define representations of a quantum monoid we need to consider it as a quantum algebra, so we should translate it by means of the (not full) embedding
{\mathbf{Alg}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
,
{\mathfrak{R}}\mapsto{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[u]
.
This is a strong monoidal functor
({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)\hookrightarrow({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
, it translates the bialgebra
({\mathfrak{R}},\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}},\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}})
to the comonoid

\displaystyle{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathfrak{R}}=({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[u],\Delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ),

\displaystyle\Delta=\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{K}}[u]},

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{K}}[u]}.

(5.1)

Component-wise we have
({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[u])_{k}={\mathfrak{R}}
,
\Delta_{k}=\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}
and
\varepsilon_{k}=\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}}
. The comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathfrak{R}}
is a quantum monoid considered as a comonoid in
({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
. Note, however, that not all the comonoid structure on the semi-connected graded algebra
{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[u]
has this form (because the embedding functor is not full).

5.1.2.

Comonoids in
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
.

A linear version of an algebraic monoid is an algebra. Its quantum analogue is a comonoid in
({\mathbf{QA}},\circ)
. A quantum semi-linear monoid is a comonoid in
({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
. For wider generality we describe comonoids in
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
, which we call
quantum algebras
.
Such comonoid is a graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=\bigoplus\limits_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
with graded homomorphisms
\Delta\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{A}}
and
\varepsilon\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathbb{K}}[u]
, their components are linear maps
\Delta_{k}\colon{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\to{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
and
\varepsilon_{k}\colon{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\to{\mathbb{K}}
satisfying the conditions
({\mathcal{A}}_{k},\Delta_{k},\varepsilon_{k})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)

\;\forall\,k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
\Delta_{k+l}(a_{l}b_{l})=\Delta_{k}(a_{k})\Delta_{l}(b_{l})

\;\forall\,k,l\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
,
a_{k}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{k}
,
b_{l}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{l}
.

By composing
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
with the embedding
{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{A}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
and evaluation
{\mathbb{K}}[u]\to{\mathbb{K}}
at
u=1
we get a comultiplication
\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}
and a counit
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}}\colon{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathbb{K}}
respectively (they are not graded). The conditions on the graded homomorphisms
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
imply that
({\mathcal{A}},\Delta_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})
is a bialgebra. Conversely, let a graded algebra
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
has a bialgebra structure:
({\mathcal{A}},\Delta_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)
, then it gives a comonoid
({\mathcal{A}},\Delta,\varepsilon)
in
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
iff

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{A}}_{k})\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}_{k}

\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}.

(5.2)

5.1.3.

Comonoids in
({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
and
({\mathbf{QA}},\circ)
.

If
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
, then it is enough to require (
5.2
) for
k=0
and
k=1
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{A}}_{0})\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}_{0},

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{A}}_{1})\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{A}}_{1}.

(5.3)

In this case
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
are completely determined by their zero and first order components
\Delta_{0}=\alpha
,
\Delta_{1}=(\alpha,t)
,
\varepsilon_{0}=\beta
,
\varepsilon_{1}=\beta\otimes\varepsilon_{V}
(see p.
4.3
for details).

In the connected case
{\mathcal{A}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
we have
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}={\mathbb{K}}
, so it is enough to impose the condition (
5.2
) for
k=1
, the graded homomorphisms
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
are uniquely determined by their first order components
\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{V}\colon V\to V\otimes V
and
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{V}\colon V\to{\mathbb{K}}
, where
V={\mathcal{A}}_{1}\in{\mathbf{Vect}}
. Then the commutativity of the diagrams (
4.3
) reduces to the requirement that these components gives a structure of coalgebra
(V,\Delta_{V},\varepsilon_{V})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
. The comonoid structure on a connected quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{A}}=TV/(R)
is given by a coalgebra
(V,\Delta_{V},\varepsilon_{V})\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Vect}},\otimes)
such that
(\Delta_{V}\otimes\Delta_{V})R\subset\sigma^{(23)}(R\otimes V^{\otimes 2}+V^{\otimes 2}\otimes R)
and
(\varepsilon_{V}\otimes\varepsilon_{V})R=0
.

5.1.4.

Quantum representations.

Let
{\mathbb{O}}=({\mathcal{A}},\Delta,\varepsilon)
be a comonoid in
({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
, that is a semi-connected quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
with a bialgebra structure
(\Delta_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})
satisfying (
5.3
). Due to the propositions
4.5
,
4.7
we have the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{FQA}}}({\mathbb{O}})
, which consists of corepresentations of
{\mathbb{O}}
on finitely generated quadratic algebras
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
. These corepresentations can be regarded as representations of the corresponding monoid in
({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
on finite-dimensional quantum linear spaces. Let us define a slightly more general notion.

Definition 5.1
.

A
quantum representation
is a corepresentation
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathbb{O}}
of a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=({\mathcal{A}},\Delta,\varepsilon)\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
on a quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
. A morphism between corepresentations
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathbb{O}}
and
\omega^{\prime}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})\to{\mathbb{O}}
is a morphism
f\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{FQA}}
such that the diagram

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(f,\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathcal{B}})}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}},f)}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 16.38895pt\omega^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}

commute. The objects
({\mathcal{B}},\omega)
, where
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
and
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathbb{O}}
is a corepresentation, form a category with such defined morphisms. We denote it by
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{FQA}}}({\mathbb{O}})
.

Note that in the case
{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c},\circ)
the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FQA}}({\mathbb{O}})
coincides with one defined in p.
3.2
, so in this case Theorem
3.8
implies that it is a full subcategory of
\mathop{\mathbf{Lcoact}}\nolimits({\mathbb{O}})
.

More correctly, we should say that the category of quantum representations is rather
\mathop{\mathbf{Rep}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{FQA}}^{\mathrm{op}}}({\mathbb{M}})
, where
{\mathbb{M}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Mon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{GrAlg}}^{\mathrm{op}},\circ)
is the monoid corresponding to the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}
. This is the category opposite to
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FQA}}({\mathbb{O}})
. The morphisms of quantum representations are reversed morphisms of corepresentations. However, it is more convenient for us to work in terms of category
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FQA}}({\mathbb{O}})
.

5.1.5.

Multiplicative Manin matrices.

An
m\times m
matrix
M=(M^{i}_{j})
with entries in a bialgebra
{\mathfrak{R}}=({\mathfrak{R}},\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}},\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}})
(or, more generally in a coalgebra) is called
multiplicative
if

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}(M^{i}_{j})=\sum_{k=1}^{m}M^{i}_{k}\otimes M^{k}_{j},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}}(M^{i}_{j})=\delta^{i}_{j},

\displaystyle i,j=1,\ldots,m

(5.8)

(see
[
Man88
, § 2.6]
,
[
Man91
, § 4.1.1]
).
For example, the formulae (
4.89
) means exactly that the universal
B
-Manin matrix
{\mathcal{M}}=({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j})
is a multiplicative
m\times m
matrix over the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits\big({\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\big)
. Recall that
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
are generators of this algebra.

Let us establish a relationship between the notions of multiplicative Manin matrix and of quantum representation. Consider a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=({\mathcal{A}},\Delta,\varepsilon)
in
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{m})
be a matrix idempotent. Any corepresentation
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathbb{O}}
of the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}
on the quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
acts on the generators by the formula

\displaystyle\omega({\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j})=M^{i}_{j},

(5.9)

where
M=(M^{i}_{j})
is a multiplicative first order
B
-Manin matrix over
{\mathcal{A}}
, that is an
m\times m
matrix with entries
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
satisfying (
5.8
) and

\displaystyle BM^{(1)}M^{(2)}(1-B)=0.

(5.10)

Conversely, any such matrix
M
defines a corepresentation of the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}
on
{\mathcal{B}}
by the formula (
5.9
).

Proof.
Any graded homomorphism
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathcal{A}}
is uniquely determined by its values
M^{i}_{j}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
on the generators
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
. The values (
5.9
) give a graded homomorphism
\omega
iff they belong to the component
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and satisfy the same commutation relations that
{\mathcal{M}}^{i}_{j}
do, i.e.the relations (
5.10
). The commutativity of the corresponding diagrams (
2.4
) is equivalent to the condition (
5.8
). ∎

Warning.
The same multiplicative first order matrix
M
defines different corepresentations (different objects of the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FQA}}({\mathbb{O}})
) on different quadratic algebras
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
and
{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})
if
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}={\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{1}
and
M
is simultaneously
B
- and
B^{\prime}
-Manin matrix. In other words, a matrix
M
is not enough to fix a corepresentation, one also needs to know the quadratic algebra
{\mathcal{B}}
(and also the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}
, of cause).

By a quantum representation of a quantum monoid we understand a corepresentation of the comonoid, obtained by embedding of the bialgebra
({\mathfrak{R}},\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}},\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}})
via
{\mathbf{Alg}}\hookrightarrow{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
. This is a corepresentation of the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathfrak{R}}=({\mathfrak{R}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[u],\Delta_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{K}}[u]},\varepsilon_{\mathfrak{R}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathbb{K}}[u]})
defined by (
5.1
). Theorem
5.2
implies in this case a one-to-one correspondence between corepresentations of
{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathfrak{R}}
on
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
and multiplicative
B
-Manin matrices over
{\mathfrak{R}}
.

A trivial but important example of quantum representation is the identical morphism
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})
. It is a corepresentation of the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}=\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})
on the object
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
. It is given by the universal
B
-Manin matrix
{\mathcal{M}}
. The corresponding coaction coincides with the coevaluation (
4.87
) for
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{B}}
.

Remark 5.3
.

In
[
RTF
, Def. 20]
the following generalisation of representation of a group was introduced. Representation of a quantum group
{\mathfrak{R}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)
on a vector space
W\in{\mathbf{FVect}}
was defined as a coalgebra homomorphism
\rho\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)^{*}\to{\mathfrak{R}}
. In the basis
(w_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
it is given by an arbitrary multiplicative first order
m\times m
matrix
M=(M^{i}_{j})
over
{\mathfrak{R}}
.
On one hand,
\rho
can be considered as a particular case of quantum representation: the corresponding corepresentation
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits(TW^{*})=T\mathop{\mathrm{\bf end}}\nolimits(W)^{*}\to{\mathbb{O}}_{\mathfrak{R}}
is given by the matrix
M
or, equivalently, by the condition
\omega_{1}=\rho
. In that sense the representations
\rho
of the quantum group
{\mathfrak{R}}
form the subcategory
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FTA}}({\mathbb{O}}_{\mathfrak{R}})
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FQA}}({\mathbb{O}}_{\mathfrak{R}})
, where
{\mathbf{FTA}}\subset{\mathbf{FQA}}
is the subcategory of finitely generated tensor algebras (it is coreflective in the sense
[
MacLane
, § 4.3]
with coreflection
{\mathbf{FQA}}\to{\mathbf{FTA}}
,
{\mathcal{B}}\mapsto T{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
).
On the other hand, any quantum representation of a comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}\in\mathop{\mathbf{Comon}}\nolimits({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
on
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
is described by a multiplicative first order matrix, which, in particular, gives corepresentation of the comonoid
{\mathbb{O}}
on
T{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\in{\mathbf{FTA}}
. We get a faithful functor
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FQA}}({\mathbb{O}})\to\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FTA}}({\mathbb{O}})
(it is also a coreflection).

5.1.6.

Morphisms of quantum representations.

Consider two quantum representations
\omega\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}})\to{\mathbb{O}}
and
\omega^{\prime}\colon\mathop{\mathrm{\bf coend}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime})\to{\mathbb{O}}
of the same
{\mathbb{O}}=({\mathcal{A}},\Delta,\varepsilon)\in({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
on quadratic algebras
{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{FQA}}
. Suppose that these algebras are defined by matrix idempotents
B\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{m}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{m})
,
B^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{m^{\prime}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}^{m^{\prime}})
, i.e.
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
,
{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})
. Due to Theorem
5.2
these corepresentations are given by multiplicative first order
B
- and
B^{\prime}
-Manin matrices
M
and
M^{\prime}
respectively:
\omega({\mathcal{M}})=M
,
\omega({\mathcal{M}}^{\prime})=M^{\prime}
, where
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}
are the universal
B
- and
B^{\prime}
-Manin matrices.

Proposition 5.4
.

Any morphism
f\colon({\mathcal{B}},\omega)\to({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
in the category
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FQA}}({\mathbb{O}})
has the form
f=f_{K}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})
for an
m\times m^{\prime}
matrix
K\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits({\mathbb{K}}^{m^{\prime}},{\mathbb{K}}^{m})
satisfying
BK^{(1)}K^{(2)}(1-B^{\prime})=0
and

\displaystyle KM^{\prime}=MK.

(5.11)

This gives a bijection between the morphisms
f\colon({\mathcal{B}},\omega)\to({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
and
(B,B^{\prime})
-Manin matrices
K
over
{\mathbb{K}}
satisfying (
5.11
).

Proof.
The graded homomorphisms
f\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})
is in one-to-one correspondence with the
(B,B^{\prime})
-Manin matrices over
{\mathbb{K}}
(see p.
4.2
). By taking the entries of the universal
(B,B^{\prime})
-Manin matrix in the left upper corner of the diagram (
5.1
) we obtain exactly the condition (
5.11
). Since these entries generate the algebra
\mathop{\mathrm{\bf cohom}}\nolimits({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{B}})
the commutativity of the diagram (
5.1
) is equivalent to (
5.11
). ∎

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}={\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{1}
. Suppose that this identification induces a morphism
f\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{FQA}}
, that is
f_{1}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}}
(this is not always true). Then
f
is a morphism
({\mathcal{B}},\omega)\to({\mathcal{B}}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
in
\mathop{\mathbf{Corep}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{FQA}}({\mathbb{O}})
iff the corepresentations
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
are given by the same matrix
M=M^{\prime}
.

Proof.
Since
f_{1}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathcal{B}}_{1}}
we have
f=f_{K}
for the identity matrix
K=1
, so the condition (
5.11
) reduces to
M=M^{\prime}
(the equation
B(1-B^{\prime})=0
is equivalent to the requirement that the matrix
K=1
defines a morphism
f_{K}\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\to{\mathfrak{X}}_{B^{\prime}}({\mathbb{K}})
in the category
{\mathbf{FQA}}
). ∎

5.1.7.

Coactions in
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
.

Let
{\mathbb{O}}
be a comonoid in
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
corresponding to a bialgebra
({\mathcal{A}},\Delta_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})
in the sense of p.
5.1
. Consider a coaction
\delta\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
of
{\mathbb{O}}
on
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{GrAlg}}
. By composing it with the embedding
{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
we obtain a coaction
\delta_{\mathcal{A}}\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
of the bialgebra
({\mathcal{A}},\Delta_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})
in the monoidal category
({\mathbf{Alg}},\otimes)
. Conversely, a coaction
\delta_{\mathcal{A}}\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}
of the bialgebra
({\mathcal{A}},\Delta_{\mathcal{A}},\varepsilon_{\mathcal{A}})
gives a coaction
\delta\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathcal{B}}
of
{\mathbb{O}}
in
({\mathbf{GrAlg}},\circ)
iff

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathcal{A}}({\mathcal{B}}_{k})\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathcal{B}}_{k},

\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}.

(5.12)

If
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}
or
{\mathcal{B}}\in{\mathbf{QA}}_{\mathrm{s}c}
then it is enough to require this condition for

k=1
only or for
k=0,1
only respectively.

Proposition 5.6
.

Each coaction
\delta\colon{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})\to{\mathcal{A}}\circ{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
has the form

\displaystyle\delta(w^{i})=\sum_{j}M^{i}_{j}\otimes w^{j},

(5.13)

where
M=(M^{i}_{j})
is a multiplicative first order
B
-Manin matrix and
(w^{j})
is the standard basis of
({\mathbb{K}}^{m})^{*}
. Any such matrix
M
define a coaction of
{\mathbb{O}}
on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
. If
\omega
is a corepresentation of
{\mathbb{O}}
on
{\mathfrak{X}}_{B}({\mathbb{K}})
given by the matrix
M
, then the diagram
